# Daily Summary: 2025-04-19

### Subitizing-Inspired_Large_Language_Models_for_Floorplanning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12076v1)
- **Authors**: Shao-Chien Lu, Chen-Chen Yeh, Hui-Lin Cho, Yu-Cheng Lin, Rung-Bin Lin
- **Abstract**: We present a novel approach to solving the floorplanning problem by leveraging fine-tuned Large Language Models (LLMs). Inspired by subitizing--the human ability to instantly and accurately count small numbers of items at a glance--we hypothesize that LLMs can similarly address floorplanning challenges swiftly and accurately. We propose an efficient representation of the floorplanning problem and introduce a method for generating high-quality datasets tailored for model fine-tuning. We fine-tune LLMs on datasets with a specified number of modules to test whether LLMs can emulate the human ability to quickly count and arrange items. Our experimental results demonstrate that fine-tuned LLMs, particularly GPT4o-mini, achieve high success and optimal rates while attaining relatively low average dead space. These findings underscore the potential of LLMs as promising solutions for complex optimization tasks in VLSI design.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary**

The paper "Subitizing-Inspired Large Language Models for Floorplanning" explores a novel approach to solving the VLSI floorplanning problem by leveraging fine-tuned Large Language Models (LLMs).  The authors draw inspiration from the human ability of "subitizing" – instantly recognizing small quantities – hypothesizing that LLMs can be similarly trained to quickly and accurately arrange modules in a floorplan. They propose an efficient representation of the floorplanning problem (slicing trees with post-order traversal encoding), introduce a method for generating training datasets, and fine-tune LLMs (both open-source models like LLaMA and Mistral, and a proprietary model, GPT40-mini). They evaluate the performance of these fine-tuned models based on success rate (generating legal slicing trees), optimal result rate (achieving zero dead space), and dead space ratio. The results show that GPT40-mini outperforms other models, achieving high success and optimal rates and lower dead space, suggesting LLMs have potential in floorplanning.

**Critical Evaluation**

**Novelty:** The application of LLMs to the floorplanning problem is somewhat novel, although the novelty is tempered by the relatively simple formulation of the problem in this work.  Using LLMs to tackle NP-hard optimization problems is a growing area, but applying a "subitizing" analogy specifically to *floorplanning* is a reasonably fresh angle. The dataset generation method based on recursive slicing and tree encoding also appears to be a unique approach designed to work well with LLMs. The choice of post-order traversal is a good, thoughtful choice to simplify model predictions.

**Significance:** The significance of the paper is limited by its scope and the level of problem abstraction. While demonstrating *some* ability of LLMs in this domain is interesting, the following points diminish its significance:

*   **Simplified Problem:** The paper makes strong simplifying assumptions (fixed module shapes, no rotations, emphasis on dead space *only*) that drastically reduce the real-world complexity of floorplanning. Real-world floorplanning involves many more constraints (wire length, thermal management, power consumption, signal integrity) which are not addressed.
*   **Limited Model Performance:** Even the "best" model, GPT40-mini, shows decreased performance (specifically, a decrease in optimal rate) as the number of modules increases. This suggests that it scales poorly. The demonstration that there is a limitation to their subitizing-like approach diminishes the significance as LLM optimization approaches will likely have to move beyond simply "counting".
*   **Lack of Comparison to Established Methods:** The paper doesn't thoroughly compare against well-established, non-LLM floorplanning algorithms. They indicate that the LLM will be used as a guide to traditional floor planners but the paper would be strengthened by showing if fine-tuned LLMs will actually improve existing methods. It is a good start to show how well it compares by itself but its final value will be judged by if it improves existing methods.
*   **Limited Generalizability:** The success is contingent on training data that is very similar to test data. The performance results are focused around module counts close to those used in training. They indicate that there is a tolerance of +/- 3 but it would require much more testing to be sure how the model would perform under more extreme situations.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Formulation:** The paper provides a clear explanation of the floorplanning problem and its representation using slicing trees.
*   **Reasonable Datasets:** The generated datasets are large enough to train LLMs, and the dataset generation method appears well-suited for the LLM task.
*   **Empirical Evaluation:** The paper includes an empirical evaluation of different LLMs, with relevant metrics.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Overly Simplified Problem:** The problem formulation misses many important aspects of real-world floorplanning.
*   **Limited Scalability:** The performance of the LLM models degrades with larger numbers of modules.
*   **Lack of Baseline Comparison:** The paper does not compare against traditional floorplanning algorithms or recent ML approaches in a rigorous way. This limits the ability to assess the true impact of LLMs.

**Score: 5**

**Rationale:** The paper presents a somewhat novel idea (applying LLMs inspired by subitizing to floorplanning). However, its contribution is ultimately limited. It tackles a heavily simplified version of the floorplanning problem, and the gains achieved do not appear to justify the computational cost and complexity of using LLMs, especially when compared to optimized conventional approaches. The limited scalability and lack of comparison to existing methods further temper the significance. The "subitizing" inspiration is an interesting framing, but the empirical results don't strongly support the idea that LLMs are truly emulating human-like instant recognition. The paper is a worthwhile initial exploration but would need substantial improvements in scope, methodology, and comparative evaluation to become a truly impactful contribution.

- **Classification**: cs.AR
- **Score**: 5/10

### Selective Demonstration Retrieval for Improved Implicit Hate Speech Detection
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12082v1)
- **Authors**: Yumin Kim, Hwanhee Lee
- **Abstract**: Hate speech detection is a crucial area of research in natural language processing, essential for ensuring online community safety. However, detecting implicit hate speech, where harmful intent is conveyed in subtle or indirect ways, remains a major challenge. Unlike explicit hate speech, implicit expressions often depend on context, cultural subtleties, and hidden biases, making them more challenging to identify consistently. Additionally, the interpretation of such speech is influenced by external knowledge and demographic biases, resulting in varied detection results across different language models. Furthermore, Large Language Models often show heightened sensitivity to toxic language and references to vulnerable groups, which can lead to misclassifications. This over-sensitivity results in false positives (incorrectly identifying harmless statements as hateful) and false negatives (failing to detect genuinely harmful content). Addressing these issues requires methods that not only improve detection precision but also reduce model biases and enhance robustness. To address these challenges, we propose a novel method, which utilizes in-context learning without requiring model fine-tuning. By adaptively retrieving demonstrations that focus on similar groups or those with the highest similarity scores, our approach enhances contextual comprehension. Experimental results show that our method outperforms current state-of-the-art techniques. Implementation details and code are available at TBD.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of detecting implicit hate speech, which is difficult due to its reliance on subtle cues, cultural context, and hidden biases. The authors propose a novel method called Adaptive Retrieval-based In-context Learning (ARIIHA) that uses in-context learning without requiring model fine-tuning. ARIIHA adaptively retrieves demonstrations by prioritizing those with similar target groups or those with high similarity scores, enhancing contextual understanding and mitigating over-sensitivity in Large Language Models (LLMs). Experimental results on the Implicit Hate Corpus (IHC) dataset demonstrate that ARIIHA outperforms existing state-of-the-art techniques.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of adaptively retrieving demonstrations based on target group similarity and BM25 score thresholds is reasonably novel. It builds upon existing work in in-context learning and retrieval-based methods but introduces a mechanism to dynamically select demonstrations based on input characteristics. The use of two distinct strategies (target group similarity and BM25 score) and the adaptive selection between them adds a layer of complexity and adaptability. The inclusion of a shortcut detection mechanism to avoid over-reliance on simple cues is also a positive innovation.

*   **Significance:** The significance lies in improving the detection of implicit hate speech, which is a critical problem with real-world implications. By addressing the over-sensitivity and dataset subjectivity issues that plague current models, ARIIHA aims to enhance the precision and robustness of hate speech detection systems. The gains demonstrated over existing methods, particularly in the context of few-shot learning, suggest that ARIIHA could be a valuable contribution.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The adaptive demonstration retrieval mechanism appears to be effective in enhancing contextual understanding.
    *   The method mitigates over-sensitivity to toxic words by considering the context of the statements.
    *   The paper provides experimental results on a relevant dataset (IHC) and demonstrates improvements over existing methods.
    *   The ablation studies help to understand the contribution of each component of ARIIHA.
    *   The inclusion of case studies illustrates the method's strengths and weaknesses.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The performance gains, while present, might not be substantial enough to warrant significant adoption. A more thorough analysis of the computational overhead and scalability of ARIIHA would be beneficial.
    *   The method relies on the accuracy of target group prediction, which could be a limitation if the target group annotations are not available or accurate.
    *   The adaptive selection mechanism depends on the tuning of thresholds. The paper describes the optimization process, but it would be valuable to investigate the sensitivity of the method to the choice of thresholds and potentially explore adaptive thresholding techniques.
    *   The description of the "shortcut" detection mechanism could be more detailed. Understanding how the system identifies and avoids relying on superficial cues is important for assessing its robustness.
    *   The paper mentions implementation details are available at TBD, suggesting they are not yet available which hinders reproducibility at the time of submission.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in hate speech detection, particularly in the area of in-context learning and retrieval-based methods. It could inspire further investigation into adaptive demonstration retrieval mechanisms and techniques for mitigating over-sensitivity in LLMs.

*   **Score:** 7

**Rationale for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and technically sound approach to a relevant problem. The experimental results demonstrate improvements over existing methods, and the ablation studies provide insights into the method's components. However, the performance gains are not overwhelmingly large, and there are some limitations related to the reliance on target group prediction, the sensitivity to threshold tuning, and the availability of implementation details.  Given these considerations, a score of 7 reflects a valuable contribution with room for further improvement and exploration.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Reasoning-Based AI for Startup Evaluation (R.A.I.S.E.): A Memory-Augmented, Multi-Step Decision Framework
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12090v1)
- **Authors**: Jack Preuveneers, Joseph Ternasky, Fuat Alican, Yigit Ihlamur
- **Abstract**: We present a novel framework that bridges the gap between the interpretability of decision trees and the advanced reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs) to predict startup success. Our approach leverages chain-of-thought prompting to generate detailed reasoning logs, which are subsequently distilled into structured, human-understandable logical rules. The pipeline integrates multiple enhancements - efficient data ingestion, a two-step refinement process, ensemble candidate sampling, simulated reinforcement learning scoring, and persistent memory - to ensure both stable decision-making and transparent output. Experimental evaluations on curated startup datasets demonstrate that our combined pipeline improves precision by 54% from 0.225 to 0.346 and accuracy by 50% from 0.46 to 0.70 compared to a standalone OpenAI o3 model. Notably, our model achieves over 2x the precision of a random classifier (16%). By combining state-of-the-art AI reasoning with explicit rule-based explanations, our method not only augments traditional decision-making processes but also facilitates expert intervention and continuous policy refinement. This work lays the foundation for the implementation of interpretable LLM-powered decision frameworks in high-stakes investment environments and other domains that require transparent and data-driven insights.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces R.A.I.S.E. (Reasoning-Based AI for Startup Evaluation), a novel framework for predicting startup success. It bridges the gap between the interpretability of decision trees and the reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs). The approach uses chain-of-thought prompting to generate reasoning logs, distills them into structured rules, and integrates several enhancements like efficient data ingestion, refinement processes, ensemble candidate sampling, reinforcement learning-based scoring, and persistent memory.  Experiments on startup datasets demonstrate improved precision and accuracy compared to a standalone LLM. The paper highlights the framework's ability to facilitate expert intervention and continuous policy refinement, leading to more transparent and data-driven insights in investment environments.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper exhibits a moderate degree of novelty. While individual components (LLMs, chain-of-thought, rule extraction, ensembling, reinforcement learning) are not entirely new, their *integration* within a comprehensive framework specifically designed for startup evaluation is a unique contribution. The modular approach and the focus on generating human-understandable decision rules distinguishes it from "black box" LLM-based prediction models. What adds a strong element of novelty is the system's focus on adaptability via policy review and expert override capability, creating a continuous feedback loop. This goes beyond simple predictive models and presents a framework for active policy management.
*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies primarily in its practical application and emphasis on interpretability in a high-stakes domain. While predictive accuracy improvements are valuable, the more significant achievement is creating a system where domain experts can understand and modify the decision-making process. This could increase trust in AI-driven investment recommendations and facilitate the integration of AI into existing VC workflows. Also, the focus on rule-based explanations with feedback loops moves beyond simple "explainable AI" towards a verifiable and adaptable AI which is much more suitable for high-stakes environments.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The framework is well-defined and modular, facilitating understanding and potential future improvements.
    *   The integration of multiple techniques (LLM prompting, rule extraction, ensembling, RL) demonstrates a thorough approach.
    *   Experimental results, while on a limited dataset, show promising improvements in precision and accuracy.
    *   Strong emphasis on interpretability and expert intervention, addressing a critical concern in AI-driven decision-making.
    *   Well-written and clearly explains the methodology.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The experimental evaluation is limited to a relatively small, curated dataset. The generalizability of the results to larger, more diverse datasets needs to be validated.
    *   The reliance on OpenAI models raises concerns about API costs and dependency on a proprietary platform. It's unclear how easily the framework could be adapted to other LLMs.
    *   The improvements in some metrics (e.g., accuracy) while appreciable are not revolutionary.
    *   The RL-based scoring mechanism, while innovative, is simulated. A more robust evaluation using real-world feedback from VC experts would strengthen the findings.
    *   Limited description on rule selection, which is a black box and is the only portion that is not necessarily interpretable.

*   **Potential Influence:**
    *   The paper could influence the development of more transparent and accountable AI systems for investment analysis.
    *   The framework's modular design could inspire similar approaches in other high-stakes domains like healthcare and finance.
    *   The emphasis on expert intervention and continuous policy refinement could promote greater trust and adoption of AI-driven decision support tools.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the moderate novelty, significant practical application, limitations of the evaluation, and strong focus on interpretability, I would rate this paper a **7**. The integration of multiple existing methods into a cohesive framework for startup evaluation is valuable, particularly with the emphasis on expert review and active policy management. However, the limited dataset, proprietary LLM dependency, and modest performance gains prevent a higher score. A larger, more diverse real-world evaluation alongside more details surrounding rule selection would be needed to significantly raise the score. That said, the paper opens interesting possibilities for AI in investment management which deserve attention.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Gauging Overprecision in LLMs: An Empirical Study
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12098v1)
- **Authors**: Adil Bahaj, Hamed Rahimi, Mohamed Chetouani, Mounir Ghogho
- **Abstract**: Recently, overconfidence in large language models (LLMs) has garnered considerable attention due to its fundamental importance in quantifying the trustworthiness of LLM generation. However, existing approaches prompt the \textit{black box LLMs} to produce their confidence (\textit{verbalized confidence}), which can be subject to many biases and hallucinations. Inspired by a different aspect of overconfidence in cognitive science called \textit{overprecision}, we designed a framework for its study in black box LLMs. This framework contains three main phases: 1) generation, 2) refinement and 3) evaluation. In the generation phase we prompt the LLM to generate answers to numerical questions in the form of intervals with a certain level of confidence. This confidence level is imposed in the prompt and not required for the LLM to generate as in previous approaches. We use various prompting techniques and use the same prompt multiple times to gauge the effects of randomness in the generation process. In the refinement phase, answers from the previous phase are refined to generate better answers. The LLM answers are evaluated and studied in the evaluation phase to understand its internal workings. This study allowed us to gain various insights into LLM overprecision: 1) LLMs are highly uncalibrated for numerical tasks 2) {\color{blue}there is no correlation between the length of the interval and the imposed confidence level, which can be symptomatic of a a) lack of understanding of the concept of confidence or b) inability to adjust self-confidence by following instructions}, {\color{blue}3)} LLM numerical precision differs depending on the task, scale of answer and prompting technique {\color{blue}4) Refinement of answers doesn't improve precision in most cases}. We believe this study offers new perspectives on LLM overconfidence and serves as a strong baseline for overprecision in LLMs.
- **Summary**: This paper introduces a framework to study *overprecision* in Large Language Models (LLMs), focusing on numerical reasoning tasks. Unlike previous work that primarily examines overestimation and relies on eliciting explicit confidence scores from LLMs, this study takes a different approach. It frames the problem as instructing LLMs to generate numerical intervals with predefined confidence levels, and then assessing if the generated intervals actually contain the true answer. The framework involves three phases: generation (generating intervals with imposed confidence), refinement (using aggregation or self-refinement techniques), and evaluation (assessing calibration using a hit metric and correlation analysis). Experiments are conducted using GPT-3.5-turbo and GPT-40-mini on financial, medical, and general knowledge datasets. The results suggest that LLMs are poorly calibrated for numerical tasks, show a weak correlation between imposed confidence and interval length, and refinement techniques provide limited gains.

**Critical Evaluation of Novelty and Significance:**

The paper's novelty lies primarily in its methodological approach to studying overconfidence. Shifting the focus from *eliciting* confidence to *imposing* it within the prompt offers a more controlled experimental setup. This allows the researchers to leverage the instruction-following capabilities of LLMs, circumventing potential biases associated with introspective self-assessment by the model. The application of cognitive science-inspired metrics like interval hit rate to evaluate LLMs' overprecision in numerical tasks is also valuable.

The paper's significance is somewhat limited. While the methodological contribution is interesting, the core findings that LLMs are poorly calibrated, and do not follow instructions for confidence well, largely reiterate existing concerns about LLM trustworthiness. However, the focus on numerical overprecision offers more granular insights into the limitations of LLMs when dealing with quantitative information, an area often crucial in real-world applications.

**Strengths:**

*   **Novel Methodology:** The prompting strategy of imposing confidence levels is a strong point, offering a different way to study overconfidence, sidestepping some limitations of previous approaches.
*   **Clear Experimental Design:** The three-phase framework is well-defined and structured, making the experiments reproducible.
*   **Cognitive Science Connection:** Drawing inspiration from cognitive science research on overprecision strengthens the theoretical grounding of the study.
*   **Detailed Analysis:** The paper analyzes the effects of different prompting techniques, refinement strategies, and datasets.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Incremental Findings:** While the methodology is novel, the conclusions reached largely confirm existing concerns about LLM calibration. The lack of significant improvement from refinement techniques is also a common finding in LLM research.
*   **Limited Scope:** The experiments focus primarily on two LLMs and a restricted range of numerical reasoning tasks.
*   **Lack of theoretical justification for some metrics:** More theory behind the ILS and DS metrics could have been included. The link to literature is superficial.
*   **Experimental design:** The impact of the random element to prompt generation has not been studied very well. As a result, some claims about how "the LLM thinks" are unsubstantiated.

**Potential Influence:**

The study can potentially influence future research on LLM calibration and trustworthiness by encouraging the exploration of alternative prompting strategies. It also provides a benchmark for evaluating LLM performance in numerical reasoning tasks. The findings highlight the need for more research into robust refinement techniques for improving LLM accuracy and calibration in numerical tasks.

Score: 6

**Rationale for Score:** The paper presents a valuable, methodologically novel approach for studying overprecision in LLMs. However, the findings themselves are somewhat incremental, confirming existing concerns. The score reflects a moderate contribution due to the methodological innovation, but is limited due to lack of substantial novel insight regarding the fundamental nature of LLMs. The results need more theoretical foundations, and the experimental design needs to be more thoroughly. More effort should be placed on explaining the results and interpreting what they tell us about LLMs.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Generalized Visual Relation Detection with Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12100v1)
- **Authors**: Kaifeng Gao, Siqi Chen, Hanwang Zhang, Jun Xiao, Yueting Zhuang, Qianru Sun
- **Abstract**: Visual relation detection (VRD) aims to identify relationships (or interactions) between object pairs in an image. Although recent VRD models have achieved impressive performance, they are all restricted to pre-defined relation categories, while failing to consider the semantic ambiguity characteristic of visual relations. Unlike objects, the appearance of visual relations is always subtle and can be described by multiple predicate words from different perspectives, e.g., ``ride'' can be depicted as ``race'' and ``sit on'', from the sports and spatial position views, respectively. To this end, we propose to model visual relations as continuous embeddings, and design diffusion models to achieve generalized VRD in a conditional generative manner, termed Diff-VRD. We model the diffusion process in a latent space and generate all possible relations in the image as an embedding sequence. During the generation, the visual and text embeddings of subject-object pairs serve as conditional signals and are injected via cross-attention. After the generation, we design a subsequent matching stage to assign the relation words to subject-object pairs by considering their semantic similarities. Benefiting from the diffusion-based generative process, our Diff-VRD is able to generate visual relations beyond the pre-defined category labels of datasets. To properly evaluate this generalized VRD task, we introduce two evaluation metrics, i.e., text-to-image retrieval and SPICE PR Curve inspired by image captioning. Extensive experiments in both human-object interaction (HOI) detection and scene graph generation (SGG) benchmarks attest to the superiority and effectiveness of Diff-VRD.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Diff-VRD, a novel approach for generalized visual relation detection (VRD) using diffusion models. Unlike existing methods that rely on pre-defined relation categories and discriminative classification, Diff-VRD models visual relations as continuous embeddings and employs a diffusion model to generate relations conditioned on subject-object pair features. The approach aims to address the semantic ambiguity inherent in visual relations, where a single interaction can be described using multiple predicate words.  The paper introduces two new evaluation metrics, text-to-image (T2I) retrieval and SPICE PR curve, to assess the model's ability to generate reasonable relations beyond the ground-truth annotations. Experiments on HOI and SGG benchmarks demonstrate the superiority and effectiveness of Diff-VRD compared to existing methods. Diff-VRD can refine the relation predictions of other VRD models as an enhancement module.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in applying diffusion models to the task of visual relation detection and addressing the inherent semantic ambiguity associated with relationships between objects in images. Previous VRD methods primarily rely on discriminative classification approaches. This generative approach, coupled with modeling relations as continuous embeddings, offers a significant departure from established techniques. It's also novel in proposing proxy tasks (T2I Retrieval, SPICE PR curve) specifically geared toward evaluating *generalized* VRD, which acknowledges reasonable outputs beyond those annotated in training sets.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from addressing limitations of traditional VRD methods. Existing approaches are often confined by pre-defined relation categories, limiting their ability to generalize to new or nuanced interactions. By generating relations and evaluating them more broadly, the authors move closer to truly comprehensive scene understanding.  The introduction of new evaluation metrics pushes the field to consider a wider range of reasonable relationships, rather than strictly adhering to the annotated ground truth. By showing that Diff-VRD can also act as a refinement module, the paper increases its utility and impact for future VRD systems.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Problem Formulation:** The paper clearly identifies the semantic ambiguity issue of visual relations and effectively frames the generalized VRD task.
    *   **Method:** The diffusion-based approach is well-motivated and technically sound. The use of continuous embeddings and cross-attention for incorporating conditional information appears to be a strong design choice.
    *   **Evaluation:** The introduction of T2I retrieval and SPICE PR curve provides a more appropriate evaluation framework for generalized VRD.
    *   **Results:** Extensive experimental results on benchmark datasets demonstrate the superiority and effectiveness of Diff-VRD.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Complexity:** Diffusion models can be computationally intensive. While the paper mentions this, a more detailed analysis of the computational cost compared to existing VRD methods would be beneficial.
    *   **Reliance on CLIP:** The model relies heavily on CLIP for feature extraction and embedding. Although CLIP is a powerful model, its biases might influence the generated relations.
    *   **Potential for Bias:** The model might still inherit biases from the training data used for the text encoder or diffusion model, leading to skewed or stereotypical relationships.
    *   **Qualitative Evaluation:** While the qualitative results showcase the ability of Diff-VRD to generate meaningful relations, a more thorough analysis of the types of relations generated and their relevance to the scene would be valuable.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research in VRD and scene understanding. The diffusion-based generative approach opens up new avenues for exploring relationships between objects.  The new evaluation metrics provide a valuable tool for assessing the quality and diversity of generated relations. Other researchers are likely to adopt the proposed evaluation metrics for generalized VRD. This may promote the development of models that output *reasonable* predictions (even if unannotated).
    The method acting as a refinement for existing models could lead to more widespread adoption of the technology.
    It could also inspire new works in the direction of open vocabulary VRD, but in a generative setting.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of visual relation detection. The application of diffusion models to this task, coupled with the focus on semantic ambiguity and the introduction of new evaluation metrics, constitutes a substantial advance.  The experimental results are compelling, and the paper is well-written and technically sound.

However, the paper is not without its limitations. The reliance on CLIP and the potential for bias, along with the computational complexity and the qualitative evaluation limitations, prevent it from achieving a higher score. It's a strong paper, but it is not a breakthrough work that completely revolutionizes the field. There are still open questions regarding how to train a less biased generative VRD model and how the model may generate inaccurate predicates with greater diversity.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Entropy-Guided Watermarking for LLMs: A Test-Time Framework for Robust and Traceable Text Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12108v1)
- **Authors**: Shizhan Cai, Liang Ding, Dacheng Tao
- **Abstract**: The rapid development of Large Language Models (LLMs) has intensified concerns about content traceability and potential misuse. Existing watermarking schemes for sampled text often face trade-offs between maintaining text quality and ensuring robust detection against various attacks. To address these issues, we propose a novel watermarking scheme that improves both detectability and text quality by introducing a cumulative watermark entropy threshold. Our approach is compatible with and generalizes existing sampling functions, enhancing adaptability. Experimental results across multiple LLMs show that our scheme significantly outperforms existing methods, achieving over 80\% improvements on widely-used datasets, e.g., MATH and GSM8K, while maintaining high detection accuracy.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel watermarking scheme for Large Language Models (LLMs) called "Entropy-Guided Watermarking."  The core idea is to dynamically adjust the watermarking process based on the estimated entropy of the generated text.  If the entropy is below a threshold, no watermark is embedded. Above the threshold, a watermark is added using preceding tokens as a seed.  The authors claim this approach improves both detectability and text quality compared to existing methods, which they argue face a trade-off between the two. The paper presents experimental results across multiple LLMs and datasets to support these claims, showing improved performance (especially on long-answer tasks) while maintaining high detection accuracy and robustness against paraphrase attacks. They theoretically prove that their algorithm is indistinguishable in terms of distribution from the non-watermarked output.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The concept of entropy-guided watermarking is a significant contribution. The idea of dynamically adapting the watermarking process based on the inherent characteristics of the generated text is a clear improvement over static watermarking schemes. This adaptation is especially important in scenarios such as few-shot learning. Further, the theoretical proofs regarding the indistinguishability of the watermark is a novel contribution.

*   **Significance:** Watermarking LLMs is a crucial area of research, driven by the need to address content traceability and potential misuse. This paper directly addresses a key weakness in existing approaches: the trade-off between text quality and robustness. If the claims hold, this method could facilitate more widespread and responsible deployment of LLMs.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The entropy-guided approach is intuitively appealing and theoretically well-motivated.
    *   The experimental results demonstrate a clear advantage over existing watermarking techniques, particularly on long-answer QA tasks and under paraphrase attacks. The fact that the method generalizes well across multiple LLMs strengthens the claims.
    *   The compatibility with existing sampling functions (ITS, BS) increases its practical applicability and enables easier integration.
    *   The theoretical proofs of indistinguishability bolsters the theoretical framework, ensuring that adaptive queries have a negligible probability of distinguishing from the non-watermarked model.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the paper provides good experimental results, further analysis on various attack vectors might be beneficial. This could include analyses on text deletion or replacement.
    *   While better than baselines, the reliance on GPT-4 for semantic similarity judgments in evaluating text quality introduces a potential subjective element. It would be good to provide more human evaluation.
    *   The paper states that the code will be released, but its absence hinders immediate reproducibility and independent verification of the results.
    *   There is a heavy reliance on comparing to just two baseline schemes, ITS and BS, which may not represent the whole landscape of watermark literature.

*   **Impact:**
    *   The method has the potential to be widely adopted in the LLM community, as it addresses a practical concern with a robust and flexible solution.
    *   It could stimulate further research into adaptive watermarking schemes that take into account the characteristics of the generated text and user interaction.
    *   By facilitating more effective watermarking, the paper could contribute to increased trust and accountability in the use of LLMs.

*   **Areas for Improvement:**
    *   Include additional experiments and analysis concerning the impact of varied entropy threshold values (lambda).
    *   Provide a more robust sensitivity analysis on various attack methods, including combinations of attacks.
    *   Perform thorough hyperparameter selection for all watermarking schemes, including the baselines, to ensure fair comparisons.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:**

The paper presents a well-motivated and innovative watermarking scheme with strong empirical support and theoretical underpinnings. The entropy-guided approach significantly advances the field by addressing the quality-detectability trade-off. However, the limitations in baseline comparisons and the lack of human evaluation and code availability, prevent a higher score. The potential impact is significant, but the score is adjusted to reflect the areas where further validation and refinement are needed. The relatively strong score reflects the significant theoretical contribution and robust empirical results, but is tempered by certain limitations regarding experimental robustness and baseline selection.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### A Diffusion-Based Framework for Terrain-Aware Remote Sensing Image Reconstruction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12112v1)
- **Authors**: Zhenyu Yu, Mohd Yamani Inda Idris, Pei Wang
- **Abstract**: Remote sensing imagery is essential for environmental monitoring, agricultural management, and disaster response. However, data loss due to cloud cover, sensor failures, or incomplete acquisition-especially in high-resolution and high-frequency tasks-severely limits satellite imagery's effectiveness. Traditional interpolation methods struggle with large missing areas and complex structures. Remote sensing imagery consists of multiple bands, each with distinct meanings, and ensuring consistency across bands is critical to avoid anomalies in the combined images. This paper proposes SatelliteMaker, a diffusion-based method that reconstructs missing data across varying levels of data loss while maintaining spatial, spectral, and temporal consistency. We also propose Digital Elevation Model (DEM) as a conditioning input and use tailored prompts to generate realistic images, making diffusion models applicable to quantitative remote sensing tasks. Additionally, we propose a VGG-Adapter module based on Distribution Loss, which reduces distribution discrepancy and ensures style consistency. Extensive experiments show that SatelliteMaker achieves state-of-the-art performance across multiple tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "SatelliteMaker," a diffusion-based framework designed for terrain-aware remote sensing image reconstruction. It addresses challenges such as cloud occlusion, missing data, and distribution discrepancies, which commonly affect satellite imagery. The method incorporates a Digital Elevation Model (DEM) as a conditional input and tailored prompts to guide the diffusion model's image generation. A VGG-Adapter module, based on a distribution loss, is proposed to reduce distribution discrepancies and ensure style consistency across different bands and timepoints.  The model is evaluated across multiple tasks and compared against existing methods, demonstrating improved performance in spatial, spectral, and temporal consistency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Problem Relevance:** The paper tackles a significant and practical problem in remote sensing: incomplete or corrupted imagery due to cloud cover, sensor failures, etc. Addressing this limitation is crucial for environmental monitoring, disaster response, and other applications.
    *   **Methodological Innovation:** The fusion of diffusion models with DEM conditioning is a promising approach. Diffusion models have shown great success in image generation, and incorporating DEM information for terrain awareness is a logical and potentially powerful extension. The VGG-Adapter module for maintaining style consistency further enhances the results.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The paper provides extensive experimental results on multiple datasets and masking strategies. The comparative analysis against existing methods (interpolation, CNNs, GANs, and previous diffusion models) demonstrates the advantages of SatelliteMaker.  The ablation study is particularly useful for understanding the contribution of the VGG-Adapter module.
    *   **Focus on Spectral and Temporal Consistency:**  The explicit focus on maintaining spectral and temporal consistency is a key strength.  This is a critical requirement for remote sensing applications that is not always emphasized in general image inpainting tasks.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Novelty of Components:** While the *combination* of diffusion models, DEM conditioning, and VGG-Adapter is novel in the context of remote sensing image reconstruction, the individual components themselves are well-established techniques. The paper might lack significant theoretical breakthroughs, which limits its potential impact.
    *   **Limited Exploration of Parameter Tuning:**  The paper describes the experimental setup but doesn't provide an in-depth analysis of the impact of different parameter settings on the performance of SatelliteMaker. A more detailed exploration of hyperparameter sensitivity would have strengthened the analysis.
    *   **Qualitative Results:** The qualitative results demonstrate the model's ability to generate plausible-looking satellite images. However, the paper could benefit from a more rigorous evaluation of the *quantitative accuracy* of the reconstructed data. For example, are the reconstructed spectral signatures accurate enough for downstream analysis (e.g., land cover classification)? The paper should directly address the importance of the application, like "climate change" and "environmental monitoring" that are only mentioned as an overview of the paper.
    *   **Limited Discussion on Computational Costs:**  Diffusion models can be computationally intensive. The paper does not thoroughly discuss the computational costs of SatelliteMaker compared to other methods. Understanding the trade-offs between accuracy and computational efficiency is important for practical applications.
    *   **Limited Discussion on Prompt Design:** The prompts used in the research must be clear with what exactly constitutes prompt engineering or prompt design.
*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper has the potential to significantly improve the usability of satellite imagery by effectively addressing data loss issues. The proposed method could be valuable for applications requiring high-resolution or high-frequency data, such as environmental monitoring, agriculture management, and disaster response.
    *   The integration of DEM information into a diffusion model opens new possibilities for terrain-aware image generation and reconstruction in the remote sensing field.
    *   The focus on spectral and temporal consistency is particularly important for quantitative remote sensing applications, where accurate spectral signatures are crucial for analysis and interpretation.

**Overall Assessment and Score:**

The paper presents a solid engineering contribution to the field of remote sensing. While the individual components of the method are not entirely novel, the combination and adaptation of these techniques to address a specific problem in remote sensing is valuable. The empirical results demonstrate a significant improvement in performance compared to existing methods. However, the lack of significant theoretical innovation, the limited exploration of parameter tuning, the somewhat limited discussion of computational costs, and lack of direct evaluation regarding the application of SatelliteMaker somewhat diminishes its overall impact. A score of 7 seems appropriate.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Clarifying Ambiguities: on the Role of Ambiguity Types in Prompting Methods for Clarification Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12113v1)
- **Authors**: Anfu Tang, Laure Soulier, Vincent Guigue
- **Abstract**: In information retrieval (IR), providing appropriate clarifications to better understand users' information needs is crucial for building a proactive search-oriented dialogue system. Due to the strong in-context learning ability of large language models (LLMs), recent studies investigate prompting methods to generate clarifications using few-shot or Chain of Thought (CoT) prompts. However, vanilla CoT prompting does not distinguish the characteristics of different information needs, making it difficult to understand how LLMs resolve ambiguities in user queries. In this work, we focus on the concept of ambiguity for clarification, seeking to model and integrate ambiguities in the clarification process. To this end, we comprehensively study the impact of prompting schemes based on reasoning and ambiguity for clarification. The idea is to enhance the reasoning abilities of LLMs by limiting CoT to predict first ambiguity types that can be interpreted as instructions to clarify, then correspondingly generate clarifications. We name this new prompting scheme Ambiguity Type-Chain of Thought (AT-CoT). Experiments are conducted on various datasets containing human-annotated clarifying questions to compare AT-CoT with multiple baselines. We also perform user simulations to implicitly measure the quality of generated clarifications under various IR scenarios.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of ambiguity in information retrieval (IR) queries and aims to improve clarification generation using Large Language Models (LLMs). The core idea is to explicitly model and integrate ambiguity types into the LLM's reasoning process. The authors propose a new prompting scheme called AMBIGUITY TYPE-CHAIN OF THOUGHT (AT-COT), which prompts LLMs to first predict ambiguity types before generating clarifying questions.  They define a simplified, action-based ambiguity type taxonomy (Semantic, Generalize, Specify) and demonstrate that AT-COT outperforms standard CoT prompting and other baselines in clarification generation and IR tasks across several datasets. The paper also includes user simulation experiments to evaluate the generated clarifications' impact on IR performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper's novelty lies primarily in the explicit modeling of ambiguity types within the LLM's clarification generation process. While previous work has explored LLM-based clarification and CoT prompting, the incorporation of an ambiguity type taxonomy to guide reasoning is a distinct contribution.  The proposed action-based taxonomy itself, while simplifying existing taxonomies, provides a practical framework for instructing LLMs.  The idea of constraining the reasoning process based on ambiguity types is a valuable contribution to improving the quality and interpretability of generated clarifications.

**Significance:**

The paper's significance stems from its potential to improve the effectiveness of conversational search and proactive dialogue systems. By explicitly addressing ambiguity, the proposed approach can generate more relevant and useful clarifications, leading to better user experiences and improved IR performance. The study provides empirical evidence supporting the benefits of ambiguity-aware prompting, offering insights into how to design more effective LLM-based clarification systems. It also demonstrates the potential of using user simulation to evaluate the quality of generated clarifications in a more realistic setting.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of ambiguity in IR and motivates the need for better clarification generation methods.
*   **Novel Approach:** AT-COT introduces a novel and potentially effective approach to LLM prompting for clarification by integrating ambiguity types.
*   **Strong Empirical Results:** The paper presents comprehensive experimental results across multiple datasets, demonstrating the superiority of AT-COT over various baselines.
*   **Well-Defined Ambiguity Taxonomy:** The authors propose a practical, action-based ambiguity type taxonomy that is suitable for LLM prompting.
*   **User Simulation:** The user simulation experiments provide valuable insights into the impact of generated clarifications on IR performance.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Ambiguity Taxonomy:**  The proposed taxonomy is relatively simple. While its simplicity is a strength for LLM integration, it may not capture the full complexity of ambiguities present in real-world queries. The lack of evaluation on alternative more fine-grained or richer taxonomies presents a limit to the impact.
*   **LLM choice:** The study utilizes Llama-3-8B, and it is not clear how well the findings generalize to other, potentially larger or more capable LLMs.
*   **User Simulation Simplifications:** The user simulation makes simplifying assumptions (e.g., cooperative users, no intent shifts) that may not fully reflect real-world user behavior. The number of turns is very small and may affect the generalization performance.
*   **Analysis Depth:** More in-depth error analysis regarding the generated clarifying questions and their impact on retrieval would strengthen the work.  For example, analyzing failure cases where AT-COT performs poorly could provide valuable insights.
*   **Correlation Study:** While there is a correlation study between CG and IR performances, the correlation coefficient is high, but the p-value is insignificant which may affect the significance of the result.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of clarification generation in IR. The AT-COT approach demonstrates a clear improvement over existing methods, and the user simulation experiments provide valuable insights into the practical impact of the research. The identified weaknesses, while noteworthy, do not significantly diminish the overall value of the work. Given the level of novelty, the comprehensive empirical evaluation, and the potential to influence future research in conversational search, a score of 7 is justified.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Anti-Aesthetics: Protecting Facial Privacy against Customized Text-to-Image Synthesis
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12129v1)
- **Authors**: Songping Wang, Yueming Lyu, Shiqi Liu, Ning Li, Tong Tong, Hao Sun, Caifeng Shan
- **Abstract**: The rise of customized diffusion models has spurred a boom in personalized visual content creation, but also poses risks of malicious misuse, severely threatening personal privacy and copyright protection. Some studies show that the aesthetic properties of images are highly positively correlated with human perception of image quality. Inspired by this, we approach the problem from a novel and intriguing aesthetic perspective to degrade the generation quality of maliciously customized models, thereby achieving better protection of facial identity. Specifically, we propose a Hierarchical Anti-Aesthetic (HAA) framework to fully explore aesthetic cues, which consists of two key branches: 1) Global Anti-Aesthetics: By establishing a global anti-aesthetic reward mechanism and a global anti-aesthetic loss, it can degrade the overall aesthetics of the generated content; 2) Local Anti-Aesthetics: A local anti-aesthetic reward mechanism and a local anti-aesthetic loss are designed to guide adversarial perturbations to disrupt local facial identity. By seamlessly integrating both branches, our HAA effectively achieves the goal of anti-aesthetics from a global to a local level during customized generation. Extensive experiments show that HAA outperforms existing SOTA methods largely in identity removal, providing a powerful tool for protecting facial privacy and copyright.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a Hierarchical Anti-Aesthetic (HAA) framework to protect facial privacy in text-to-image (T2I) synthesis.  It tackles the problem from an aesthetic perspective, aiming to degrade the generation quality of maliciously customized models. The HAA framework consists of two branches: Global Anti-Aesthetics (GAA), which reduces overall image quality, and Local Anti-Aesthetics (LAA), which disrupts facial identity.  The key idea is to leverage the connection between human perception of image quality and aesthetic properties, and actively sabotage it.  The method uses anti-aesthetic reward mechanisms and losses at both global and local levels to guide adversarial perturbations.  Extensive experiments demonstrate the superiority of HAA over existing methods in identity removal while maintaining image utility.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The idea of using anti-aesthetics to protect facial privacy is relatively novel.  Existing methods primarily focus on direct perturbation of the image to disrupt the fine-tuning process. The paper's key contribution is in recognizing and exploiting the connection between aesthetic properties and human perception to degrade image quality in a targeted manner.  The hierarchical approach, separating global and local aesthetics, is also a novel element. While adversarial attacks and manipulations of latent spaces in diffusion models are well-explored, the specific combination of aesthetic degradation and a hierarchical architecture for privacy is a distinct contribution.

* **Significance:**  The potential impact of this work is significant.  With the increasing accessibility and sophistication of customized diffusion models, the risks to personal privacy and copyright protection are growing. An effective and subtle method for preventing misuse of these models is crucial. The paper presents a promising approach to counteract these threats, making custom T2I models safer to use, offering an alternative to more disruptive techniques.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Novel approach:** Using anti-aesthetics for privacy protection is a creative and relatively unexplored direction.
    * **Hierarchical framework:** Decomposing the problem into global and local components is well-reasoned and effective.
    * **Strong experimental results:**  The experiments show significant improvements over existing state-of-the-art methods in terms of reducing facial detection success rate, reducing face similarity, and increasing FID, while also demonstrating robustness to different diffusion model versions and perturbations.
    * **Good Ablation studies:** the ablation studies demonstrated the importance of both Global and Local Anti-Aesthetic components.
    * **Black box setup:** They demonstrate the approach works for other DM versions and settings.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Reliance on Aesthetic Reward Models:** The performance heavily relies on the quality and robustness of the aesthetic reward models. If these models are biased or vulnerable to adversarial attacks themselves, the entire framework could be compromised. Further research could focus on making the system more robust to weaknesses or biases within the reward model.
    * **Limited Generalizability of "Aesthetics":** The definition of "aesthetics" used is derived from a specific dataset (VisionRewardDB-Image). It is crucial to examine the cultural and societal biases within this dataset and how these biases might affect the system's effectiveness across diverse demographics and artistic styles.
    * **Qualitative Results:** While the quantitative results are compelling, the qualitative results, or visual examples of protected images, should be stronger to illustrate the efficacy in preserving original image content while preventing misuse.
    * **Subtlety vs. Effectiveness Trade-off:** There's a potential trade-off between the subtlety of the adversarial noise and the effectiveness of the privacy protection.  Extremely subtle noise might not be sufficient to prevent determined attackers. A study of this trade-off would strengthen the paper.
    * **Scalability**:  The paper focuses on a limited number of identities. Assessing the method's scalability to larger datasets and more diverse identities is critical.

* **Overall:** The paper offers a novel and potentially significant contribution to privacy protection in the context of customized diffusion models. The strengths of the proposed method, particularly the novel approach and strong empirical results, outweigh its weaknesses. While there are valid concerns regarding the dependence on aesthetic reward models and potential biases, the proposed solution is innovative.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**  The paper deserves a 7.5 because of the novelty of the idea and the solid experimental evidence supporting its effectiveness. The hierarchical framework is well-designed, and the results are demonstrably better than existing methods. However, the concerns regarding reliance on potentially biased aesthetic reward models and potential biases limit a higher rating. Further investigation in the direction of the black box setup is also desired, with a more wide array of pre-trained models tested. Finally, while being more qualitative, the protective noise is too effective to still be considered as minimally invasive in its degradation of the generated images. Addressing these weaknesses in future work could significantly increase the impact of this research.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Multilingual Contextualization of Large Language Models for Document-Level Machine Translation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12140v1)
- **Authors**: Miguel Moura Ramos, Patrick Fernandes, Sweta Agrawal, André F. T. Martins
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated strong performance in sentence-level machine translation, but scaling to document-level translation remains challenging, particularly in modeling long-range dependencies and discourse phenomena across sentences and paragraphs. In this work, we propose a method to improve LLM-based long-document translation through targeted fine-tuning on high-quality document-level data, which we curate and introduce as DocBlocks. Our approach supports multiple translation paradigms, including direct document-to-document and chunk-level translation, by integrating instructions both with and without surrounding context. This enables models to better capture cross-sentence dependencies while maintaining strong sentence-level translation performance. Experimental results show that incorporating multiple translation paradigms improves document-level translation quality and inference speed compared to prompting and agent-based methods.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of document-level machine translation (MT) using large language models (LLMs).  It introduces DOCBLOCKS, a new dataset curated for document-level contextualization, which comprises full documents and contextual segments from diverse domains. The authors propose a method to fine-tune sentence-level LLMs on DOCBLOCKS, supporting various translation paradigms like direct document-to-document and chunk-level translation.  This fine-tuning approach leverages instructions both with and without surrounding context.  Experimental results demonstrate that the proposed method improves document-level translation quality and inference speed compared to prompting and agent-based approaches. The paper also evaluates the impact of various factors like chunk size, prompt tuning, and quality aware chunking.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the creation and utilization of the DOCBLOCKS dataset specifically designed for document-level contextualization in MT. While fine-tuning and instruction tuning are not new, the targeted application and the curated dataset enhance the ability of pre-trained LLMs to perform document-level translation. The exploration of different translation paradigms (document-to-document, chunking with/without context) is also a valuable aspect. However, the fine-tuning strategy itself is not particularly groundbreaking. The integration of quality-aware chunking through methods such as COMET has been explored separately but its integration within the document level translation is incremental. The model is based on existing models such as TOWER, EUROLLM and Qwen.

*   **Significance:** Document-level MT is a crucial area, and the paper makes a solid contribution by providing a resource and method to improve LLMs in this domain. The experiments demonstrate quantifiable improvements in document-level BLEU and COMET scores compared to sentence-level baselines, demonstrating the value of contextualization. The comparison with prompting and agent-based approaches provides a useful benchmark. The dataset DOCBLOCKS appears to address a key bottleneck in the field – the lack of high-quality training data for document-level MT. The evaluation focuses on relevant metrics such as d-BLEU, d-COMET, and LTCR. The ablation studies provide insight into the dataset curation.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Well-defined problem and clear goals.
    *   Creation of a valuable dataset (DOCBLOCKS) that addresses data scarcity.
    *   Systematic comparison with baselines, including prompting and agent-based methods.
    *   Analysis of various factors influencing translation quality.
    * The impact of context awareness is explored at various levels.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The fine-tuning approach itself is not particularly novel.
    *   The gains over the QWen2.5-72B-Instruct may not seem that significant given the smaller size of the used MT finetuned LLMs.
    *   The paper does not present any qualitative examples of the improvements introduced by DOCBLOCKS.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence the field by:
    *   Providing a benchmark dataset (DOCBLOCKS) for document-level MT research.
    *   Encouraging further research into fine-tuning strategies for contextualizing LLMs for MT.
    *   Stimulating the development of more sophisticated evaluation metrics for document-level MT.

**Score:** 7

**Rationale:** The paper offers a valuable dataset and demonstrates a practical approach to improving document-level MT with LLMs. While the technical novelty of the fine-tuning method itself is not exceptionally high, the DOCBLOCKS dataset, the thorough experimental evaluation, and the comparison to competing methods contribute significantly to the field. There is room for further innovation in the fine-tuning strategies or alternative model architectures tailored to document-level context, and the models are compared against the significantly larger Qwen. Hence, the score reflects the solid contribution, but also the scope for further innovation in this area.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Mapping Controversies Using Artificial Intelligence: An Analysis of the Hamas-Israel Conflict on YouTube
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12177v1)
- **Authors**: Victor Manuel Hernandez Lopez, Jaime E. Cuellar
- **Abstract**: This article analyzes the Hamas-Israel controversy through 253,925 Spanish-language YouTube comments posted between October 2023 and January 2024, following the October 7 attack that escalated the conflict. Adopting an interdisciplinary approach, the study combines the analysis of controversies from Science and Technology Studies (STS) with advanced computational methodologies, specifically Natural Language Processing (NLP) using the BERT (Bidirectional Encoder Representations from Transformers) model. Using this approach, the comments were automatically classified into seven categories, reflecting pro-Palestinian, pro-Israeli, anti- Palestinian, anti-Israeli positions, among others. The results show a predominance of pro- Palestinian comments, although pro-Israeli and anti-Palestinian comments received more "likes." This study also applies the agenda-setting theory to demonstrate how media coverage significantly influences public perception, observing a notable shift in public opinion, transitioning from a pro- Palestinian stance to a more critical position towards Israel. This work highlights the importance of combining social science perspectives with technological tools in the analysis of controversies, presenting a methodological innovation by integrating computational analysis with critical social theories to address complex public opinion phenomena and media narratives.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper, along with a novelty and significance score.

**Summary:**

The paper "Mapping Controversies Using Artificial Intelligence: An Analysis of the Hamas-Israel Conflict on YouTube" uses Natural Language Processing (NLP) with the BERT model to analyze 253,925 Spanish-language YouTube comments posted between October 2023 and January 2024, following the October 7th attack.  It categorizes comments into seven categories reflecting pro-Palestinian, pro-Israeli, anti-Palestinian, and anti-Israeli positions. The study finds a prevalence of pro-Palestinian comments but notes that pro-Israeli and anti-Palestinian comments receive more likes. It applies agenda-setting theory to examine how media coverage affects public perception, observing a shift from a pro-Palestinian stance to a more critical view of Israel. The paper aims to combine social science perspectives with technological tools to provide a methodological innovation for analyzing controversies and their related public opinion.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper's central premise, combining computational methods (NLP, BERT) with social science theory (Controversy Mapping, Agenda-Setting), holds initial promise. However, upon closer inspection, the actual novelty and significance are debatable.

**Strengths:**

*   **Timely Topic:** Addressing the contentious Hamas-Israel conflict is relevant and reflects an important contemporary issue in public discourse.
*   **Interdisciplinary Approach:**  Attempting to bridge the gap between computational methods and critical social science is a laudable aim.
*   **Large Dataset:**  Analyzing a dataset of over 250,000 comments offers a significant scale for exploring online discourse.
*   **Clear Methodology:** The paper provides a relatively detailed explanation of its data collection, pre-processing, annotation, and modeling procedures.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Novelty:** The application of NLP techniques, particularly BERT, to analyze online discourse is not inherently new. While the context (Hamas-Israel conflict) is specific, the *methodological application* is not groundbreaking. Other studies have used similar techniques on other contentious topics.
*   **Superficial Integration of Theory:**  The integration of social science theory appears somewhat superficial. Agenda-setting is mentioned, but the analysis doesn't deeply explore the nuances of how specific media frames influenced the different comment categories. The analysis only touches on the relationship between the timeline of events, media coverage, and overall trends in the comments.
*   **Oversimplified Categorization:**  The seven-category classification (Pro-Palestinian, Pro-Israeli, Anti-Palestinian, Anti-Israeli, etc.) seems somewhat simplistic and may not capture the full complexity and nuances of opinions expressed in the comments. A more in-depth qualitative analysis of a subset of comments might have revealed more nuanced positions.
*   **Lack of Qualitative Validation:** While the paper notes the use of random sampling to prevent bias, there's little discussion of qualitative validation. A more detailed qualitative examination of the comment categorization and resulting interpretations is warranted to ensure a more rigorous approach.
*   **Annotation Challenges:**  The discussion regarding the lack of Anti-Hamas category and the difficulty of correctly classifying Anti-Hamás comments underscores a limitation. This could be solved by a more in-depth annotation process and a more robust set of guidelines, especially by considering the nuances of the language.
*   **Limited Discussion of Ethical Considerations:** While the topic is sensitive, a richer discussion about the ethical implications of analyzing online discourse related to a conflict, especially concerning potential biases in the data or model, and the impact of findings would have been worthwhile.
*   **Lack of comparative analysis**: A significant improvement would have been to compare the results found on YouTube with results found on other social media (e.g. Reddit) to see if the results are generalizable or specific to YouTube.

**Significance:**

While the paper addresses a current and important topic, its contribution to the field is moderate at best. It demonstrates the feasibility of using NLP for analyzing online discourse related to conflict but falls short of offering significant methodological innovation or providing deeply insightful findings. The paper primarily describes the overall trends and doesn't delve into the underlying reasons for observed shifts in public sentiment.

**Score: 5**

**Justification:**

The paper earns a 5 because it is a well-executed, but largely incremental contribution. The methodology is sound but not particularly innovative. The findings are interesting but don't offer dramatically new insights or challenge existing understandings of the conflict or the dynamics of online discourse. The superficial integration of social science theory and ethical considerations, coupled with somewhat simplistic categorization, weaken the overall significance. The study has value in documenting the prevalence of different viewpoints in YouTube comments, however, given the current state of literature, the novelty is limited. Stronger emphasis on the limitations and suggestions of future work related to improving NLP-based sentiment analysis, such as improving annotation, may elevate its significance in the long run.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 5/10

### Trusting CHATGPT: how minor tweaks in the prompts lead to major differences in sentiment classification
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12180v1)
- **Authors**: Jaime E. Cuellar, Oscar Moreno-Martinez, Paula Sofia Torres-Rodriguez, Jaime Andres Pavlich-Mariscal, Andres Felipe Mican-Castiblanco, Juan Guillermo Torres-Hurtado
- **Abstract**: One fundamental question for the social sciences today is: how much can we trust highly complex predictive models like ChatGPT? This study tests the hypothesis that subtle changes in the structure of prompts do not produce significant variations in the classification results of sentiment polarity analysis generated by the Large Language Model GPT-4o mini. Using a dataset of 100.000 comments in Spanish on four Latin American presidents, the model classified the comments as positive, negative, or neutral on 10 occasions, varying the prompts slightly each time. The experimental methodology included exploratory and confirmatory analyses to identify significant discrepancies among classifications. The results reveal that even minor modifications to prompts such as lexical, syntactic, or modal changes, or even their lack of structure impact the classifications. In certain cases, the model produced inconsistent responses, such as mixing categories, providing unsolicited explanations, or using languages other than Spanish. Statistical analysis using Chi-square tests confirmed significant differences in most comparisons between prompts, except in one case where linguistic structures were highly similar. These findings challenge the robustness and trust of Large Language Models for classification tasks, highlighting their vulnerability to variations in instructions. Moreover, it was evident that the lack of structured grammar in prompts increases the frequency of hallucinations. The discussion underscores that trust in Large Language Models is based not only on technical performance but also on the social and institutional relationships underpinning their use.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've reviewed the provided OCR output of the paper. Here's a summary, a critical evaluation, and a score:

**Summary**

The paper investigates the robustness of Large Language Models (LLMs), specifically GPT-4o mini, in sentiment analysis. The central research question explores whether subtle variations in prompt construction significantly alter the results of sentiment classification. The authors tested this hypothesis by classifying 100,000 YouTube comments (in Spanish) on four Latin American presidents using ten different prompts with minor lexical, syntactic, modal, and structural variations. The methodology combined exploratory analyses (PCA, Levenshtein distance) with a confirmatory chi-squared test to identify significant discrepancies between classifications resulting from different prompts. The results revealed that even minor prompt modifications can lead to statistically significant differences in sentiment classifications, demonstrating the fragility of LLMs in this task. Furthermore, prompt structure influenced the occurrence of the model hallucinating, giving inconsistent, or unwanted responses. The paper then discusses the implications of these findings for trust in LLMs, arguing that confidence should rely on both technical robustness *and* the social/institutional context of their use.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   *Empirical Rigor:* The paper uses a large dataset (100,000 comments) and applies quantitative methods to test its hypothesis. The use of both exploratory and confirmatory statistical analyses is a strength.
    *   *Clear Research Question:* The paper focuses on a highly relevant and timely question about the reliability of LLMs. It addresses the replicability and robustness challenges in natural language processing.
    *   *Well-Defined Prompt Variations:* Systematically varying the prompts across different types (lexical, syntactic, modal, structural) is well-designed and allows isolating the impact of specific prompt features.
    *   *Multifaceted Trust Discussion:* The paper moves beyond purely technical assessments to include sociological perspectives on trust, acknowledging both technical and the larger social context.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   *Limited Scope:* While valuable, the study focuses only on sentiment analysis and only in Spanish. Generalizability to other NLP tasks (e.g., text summarization, translation) or languages needs further exploration. Also, only YouTube comments were tested, limiting the generalizability to other social media and online settings.
    *   *Incremental Novelty:* While contributing to the existing literature on prompt engineering, the empirical finding that prompts *do* matter is not entirely new. However, the systematic examination of the *degree* to which subtle prompts matter, as well as the incorporation of institutional trust and a sociological understanding of trust, helps enhance its novelty.
    *   *Chi-Squared Limitation:*  While the Chi-squared test identifies significant differences, it doesn't specify the *nature* or *magnitude* of those differences. Further analysis to examine the specifics of disagreement in classification is needed. The study also lacks a manual check of results (a *ground truth*), which leaves open the possibility that, while different, some prompts produce more "correct" responses.
    *   *Model Choice:* Using GPT-4o mini is appropriate given its capabilities, using a larger model or a more recently released model may yield differing results.
    *   *Lack of Comparative Analysis:* It would have been strengthened by comparisons to other LLMs, to explore how generalized these findings are.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper is significant because it empirically demonstrates the sensitivity of LLMs to subtle prompt variations, highlighting the need for caution in relying on these models for tasks where consistency and robustness are crucial. The finding of LLM "fragility" is particularly concerning in contexts with limited expertise or resources.
    *   The work contributes to the growing body of literature on prompt engineering, but does so in a manner that raises important questions about trust in the process and output of LLMs.
    *   The study also has practical implications for LLM developers and users by emphasizing the importance of careful prompt design.

**Overall**

The paper presents a valuable empirical investigation into LLM robustness that is relevant to current concerns regarding the reliability of these models. While the core finding (prompts matter) isn't groundbreaking, the study's systematic approach, large dataset, consideration of different kinds of prompts, and theoretical framework justify a solid score.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:**
