By highlighting the importance of effective semantic diversity, it encourages researchers to develop metrics and training techniques that prioritize both quality and meaningful variety in outputs. The findings on model size and parameter efficiency could guide the development of more resource-efficient LLMs for specific applications.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper is a significant contribution because it redefines diversity metrics in a way that is more useful for real-world applications, particularly in contexts that require high-quality outputs. The framework is well-defined, the experimental results are compelling, and the analysis offers novel insights. While there are limitations regarding the generalizability of the task domain and the reliance on program execution, the core ideas and findings have the potential to substantially impact the field. As such, I'd assign a score that recognizes both the strengths and weaknesses.

### Memorization vs. Reasoning: Updating LLMs with New Knowledge
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12523v1)
- **Authors**: Aochong Oliver Li, Tanya Goyal
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) encode vast amounts of pre-trained knowledge in their parameters, but updating them as real-world information evolves remains a challenge. Existing methodologies and benchmarks primarily target entity substitutions, failing to capture the full breadth of complex real-world dynamics. In this paper, we introduce Knowledge Update Playground (KUP), an automatic pipeline for simulating realistic knowledge updates reflected in an evidence corpora. KUP's evaluation framework includes direct and indirect probes to both test memorization of updated facts and reasoning over them, for any update learning methods. Next, we present a lightweight method called memory conditioned training (MCT), which conditions tokens in the update corpus on self-generated "memory" tokens during training. Our strategy encourages LLMs to surface and reason over newly memorized knowledge at inference. Our results on two strong LLMs show that (1) KUP benchmark is highly challenging, with the best CPT models achieving $<2\%$ in indirect probing setting (reasoning) and (2) MCT training significantly outperforms prior continued pre-training (CPT) baselines, improving direct probing (memorization) results by up to $25.4\%$.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Memorization vs. Reasoning: Updating LLMs with New Knowledge":

**Summary**

The paper addresses the challenge of updating Large Language Models (LLMs) with new knowledge.  Current benchmarks primarily focus on entity substitutions, which the authors argue is insufficient to capture real-world dynamic information updates.  They introduce Knowledge Update Playground (KUP), an automated pipeline for generating realistic, though fictitious, knowledge updates. KUP includes direct and indirect probing to test both memorization of updated facts and reasoning over them.  They also present memory conditioned training (MCT), a training method that conditions update tokens on self-generated "memory" tokens, encouraging the LLM to surface and reason over newly memorized knowledge. Experiments on Llama-3.1-8B and Mistral-7B show that KUP is challenging and that MCT outperforms prior continued pre-training (CPT) baselines, improving direct probing by up to 25.4%. A major finding is that all CPT approaches fail in the indirect probing setting.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces a valuable new benchmark, KUP, which is more realistic than existing entity-substitution frameworks. The focus on generating fictitious updates for future-proofing the benchmark is also a good idea, although the generation process itself relies on GPT-4, potentially inheriting biases. MCT is a reasonably novel approach, conditioning training on the LLM's own memory of a fact. While prior work has touched on similar ideas, the application within a CPT framework and the specific methodology are distinct.
*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper lies in its more realistic benchmark and a method that shows improvements in memorization, but fails to solve reasoning. The finding that current methods fail in indirect probing highlights the limitations of current update strategies and underscores the need for further research into reasoning over updated knowledge. The method’s improved performance over standard CPT is also a valuable contribution.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The KUP benchmark is a significant contribution. It fills a gap in the existing benchmarks for knowledge updating and is designed to be more comprehensive and realistic.
    *   The MCT method shows promise in improving the memorization of updated knowledge and its results on the direct probing task show an improvement over standard CPT baselines.
    *   The analysis of existing benchmarks and the characteristics of knowledge updates are well-reasoned and provide valuable insights.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The indirect probing remains a major weakness. The fact that none of the CPT approaches perform well in this setting indicates that the "reasoning" aspect of knowledge update is still a largely unsolved problem. It would be valuable to probe on why the indirect probing is so low.
    *   The reliance on GPT-4 for generating KUP data introduces potential biases. While the authors address this with verification steps, it's still a concern.
    *   The scope is limited to two models and a single epoch of training. Further experimentation with different models, training lengths, and hyperparameter tuning would strengthen the findings.
    *   The manual design of the indirect probes is time-consuming and raises questions about generalizability and scalability.
*   **Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of **7** to this paper.

*   The positive aspects are the introduction of a new more realistic benchmark (KUP) and the MCT method, showing promising results in memorization with direct probing.
*   The paper, however, doesn’t solve the challenging problem of *reasoning* over updated knowledge and all CPT methods failed the indirect probing settings.
*   Finally, despite attempts to be objective by verifying facts, the use of GPT-4 and GPT-4-MINI in the data generation stage may induce biases into the dataset.

The paper is a valuable addition to the field of knowledge updating for LLMs, highlighting the need for approaches that go beyond simple memorization and address the reasoning challenges. KUP can serve as a useful benchmark for future research, and MCT provides a promising starting point for improving memorization performance.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 0/10

### Generalization through variance: how noise shapes inductive biases in diffusion models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12532v1)
- **Authors**: John J. Vastola
- **Abstract**: How diffusion models generalize beyond their training set is not known, and is somewhat mysterious given two facts: the optimum of the denoising score matching (DSM) objective usually used to train diffusion models is the score function of the training distribution; and the networks usually used to learn the score function are expressive enough to learn this score to high accuracy. We claim that a certain feature of the DSM objective -- the fact that its target is not the training distribution's score, but a noisy quantity only equal to it in expectation -- strongly impacts whether and to what extent diffusion models generalize. In this paper, we develop a mathematical theory that partly explains this 'generalization through variance' phenomenon. Our theoretical analysis exploits a physics-inspired path integral approach to compute the distributions typically learned by a few paradigmatic under- and overparameterized diffusion models. We find that the distributions diffusion models effectively learn to sample from resemble their training distributions, but with 'gaps' filled in, and that this inductive bias is due to the covariance structure of the noisy target used during training. We also characterize how this inductive bias interacts with feature-related inductive biases.
- **Summary**: This paper presents a theoretical analysis of generalization in diffusion models, focusing on how the variance of the noisy target used during training (the 'proxy score') shapes inductive biases. The authors argue that the standard denoising score matching (DSM) objective's target being a noisy version of the true score, rather than the true score itself, is key to enabling generalization beyond memorization. They develop a physics-inspired framework based on the Martin-Siggia-Rose (MSR) path integral to compute the distributions learned by under- and overparameterized diffusion models. Their analysis reveals that learned distributions resemble training data but with gaps filled in, attributing this to the covariance structure of the proxy score. The paper also explores how this inductive bias interacts with feature-related biases.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's central argument – that the variance of the noisy training target is a key driver of generalization in diffusion models – is a significant contribution. Most prior theoretical work has focused on convergence to a known ground truth, given infinite data. This paper directly addresses the crucial, but less explored, question of how diffusion models generalize given a finite training set. The use of the MSR path integral to approximate the learned distribution is a novel technique in this context and provides a valuable tool for analyzing these models. The detailed consideration of the six factors influencing generalization is insightful and provides a more holistic view of the problem. The analysis of toy models (linear and lazy infinite-width neural networks) is used effectively as testbeds to explore the interaction of these factors.

*   **Significance:** Understanding generalization in diffusion models is of paramount importance as these models gain wider adoption in various applications. The paper offers a framework for characterizing the inductive biases inherent in diffusion models. These insights can inform future model design choices (e.g., selection of forward processes, loss weighting strategies, and architectures) and improve the quality of generated samples.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The theoretical framework is well-motivated and rigorously developed.
    *   The paper addresses a key question in diffusion model research that is relatively unexplored in the theoretical domain.
    *   The analysis identifies specific factors (noisy objective, forward process, etc.) impacting generalization, providing a roadmap for further research.
    *   The use of the MSR path integral offers a powerful analytical tool.
    *   The illustrative examples (linear models, NTK models) enhance understanding and provide validation of the theory.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The analysis relies on approximations (e.g., neglecting higher-order cumulants in the MSR path integral).  While these are stated and justified, their potential impact is not fully understood.
    *   The scope is limited to unconditional, non-latent models, which restricts the applicability of the results to more complex, practically relevant scenarios.  The text conditioning (e.g. in Stable Diffusion) is essential in the diffusion models world.
    *   The theoretical analysis is inherently complex, potentially limiting accessibility for researchers without a strong background in statistical physics or stochastic calculus.
    *   While the paper offers explanations for some existing empirical findings, it would be significantly strengthened by predicting novel phenomena that can be experimentally verified.
    *   Some of the approximations in the supplementary material seems a little hand-wavy.
    *   The theoretical part would have greatly benefitted from a more clearly stated set of simplifying assumptions which led to the results, and a discussion about them.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by providing a new theoretical perspective on generalization in diffusion models. It could stimulate further research on characterizing the inductive biases induced by various design choices and developing more effective training strategies. The MSR path integral framework might become a standard tool for analyzing diffusion models.

**Rationale for Score:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the theoretical understanding of generalization in diffusion models. The noisy target-variance perspective is novel and insightful, and the MSR path integral-based framework provides a potentially powerful tool for analysis. While the scope is limited to unconditional models, the theoretical framework laid out in this paper creates a foundation for further investigations for generative diffusion models. However, the reliance on approximations and the absence of novel experimental predictions limit the immediate impact. The complexity of the analysis may also hinder its widespread adoption.

### Knowledge Acquisition on Mass-shooting Events via LLMs for AI-Driven Justice
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12545v1)
- **Authors**: Benign John Ihugba, Afsana Nasrin, Ling Wu, Lin Li, Lijun Qian, Xishuang Dong
- **Abstract**: Mass-shooting events pose a significant challenge to public safety, generating large volumes of unstructured textual data that hinder effective investigations and the formulation of public policy. Despite the urgency, few prior studies have effectively automated the extraction of key information from these events to support legal and investigative efforts. This paper presented the first dataset designed for knowledge acquisition on mass-shooting events through the application of named entity recognition (NER) techniques. It focuses on identifying key entities such as offenders, victims, locations, and criminal instruments, that are vital for legal and investigative purposes. The NER process is powered by Large Language Models (LLMs) using few-shot prompting, facilitating the efficient extraction and organization of critical information from diverse sources, including news articles, police reports, and social media. Experimental results on real-world mass-shooting corpora demonstrate that GPT-4o is the most effective model for mass-shooting NER, achieving the highest Micro Precision, Micro Recall, and Micro F1-scores. Meanwhile, o1-mini delivers competitive performance, making it a resource-efficient alternative for less complex NER tasks. It is also observed that increasing the shot count enhances the performance of all models, but the gains are more substantial for GPT-4o and o1-mini, highlighting their superior adaptability to few-shot learning scenarios.
- **Summary**: Okay, I'll provide a summary and critical evaluation of the paper based on the provided text.

**Summary**

The paper addresses the challenge of extracting and structuring information from unstructured text data related to mass-shooting events. It introduces a dataset specifically designed for knowledge acquisition on these events using Named Entity Recognition (NER) techniques. The authors leverage Large Language Models (LLMs) like GPT-3.5, GPT-4o, and 01-mini using few-shot prompting to identify key entities such as offenders, victims, locations, and criminal instruments. Experimental results on a real-world mass-shooting corpus show that GPT-4o is the most effective model for this task, achieving the highest Micro Precision, Micro Recall, and Micro F1-scores, while 01-mini offers a resource-efficient alternative. The paper highlights the potential of LLMs for AI-driven justice, supporting investigations, policy-making, and predictive analyses related to mass shootings. The authors emphasize the efficiency gains from few-shot learning and present a structured dataset tailored for mass-shooting event analysis.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The paper claims to present the *first* dataset specifically designed for knowledge acquisition on mass-shooting events using NER techniques. If accurate, this is a significant claim. The creation of a structured dataset focused on this specific domain fills a gap, given the importance of understanding and addressing mass shootings. The application of LLMs to this dataset is also a contribution, although LLMs have been used for NER in other domains. The specific use-case and curated dataset, if novel, lend to the novelty.

*   **Significance:** Mass shootings represent a major public safety concern. Tools to analyze and understand these events are highly valuable. If this dataset and NER pipeline help in investigations, policy-making, or predictive analyses, it could have a real-world impact. Structuring this knowledge fills a real gap.  However, the degree of impact will depend on the accessibility, quality, and broad adoption of the dataset and the developed NER pipeline.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Targeted Dataset:** Focusing on mass shootings and creating a dataset with detailed annotations is a significant strength. The categorization into 41 subcategories is comprehensive.
    *   **LLM Evaluation:** Comparing different LLMs (GPT-3.5, GPT-4o, 01-mini) provides valuable insights into their suitability for this specific NER task.
    *   **Few-Shot Learning:** Leveraging few-shot prompting is practical, especially when data is limited. It improves efficiency and scalability.
    *   **Clear Experimental Results:** The paper reports clear and easily comparable experimental results using standard NER metrics. The analysis of precision, recall, and F1-score for each model is insightful.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dataset Size and Source:** While the paper mentions 153 events, the reliance on a single source (MotherJones) could introduce bias or limit the generalizability of the findings. Details about data balancing and potential biases in the MotherJones dataset are important considerations.
    *   **LLM Selection:** The choice of only three OpenAI LLMs could be seen as a limitation. Exploring other open-source LLMs could broaden the impact and accessibility of the research.
    *   **Accessibility and Validation:** The paper doesn't specify how the dataset will be made publicly available. The utility needs to be tested by law enforcement or other stakeholders. The dataset should be subject to external validation to ensure high quality.

*   **Potential Influence:** The dataset and NER pipeline could influence research on mass shootings, AI-driven justice, and the application of LLMs to specialized domains. It could inspire further development of AI tools for public safety. However, its influence will depend on its availability, quality, and integration into real-world applications.

*   **Caveats**: The results presented are limited by the specific LLMs and dataset used. Generalizability to other LLMs or event datasets remains to be demonstrated. The absence of error analysis or qualitative assessment of the NER results is also noted.

**Justification of Score:**

Given the paper's focus on a critical societal issue, the claim of a novel dataset, and thorough LLM experimentation, it has potential for positive impact. However, the reliance on a single data source, the limited scope of LLM comparisons, and questions around data accessibility and validation temper its overall significance. I rate the paper as a **7**. This acknowledges the novelty and significance of the work while also recognizing the limitations mentioned above.

### ELAB: Extensive LLM Alignment Benchmark in Persian Language
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12553v1)
- **Authors**: Zahra Pourbahman, Fatemeh Rajabi, Mohammadhossein Sadeghi, Omid Ghahroodi, Somaye Bakhshaei, Arash Amini, Reza Kazemi, Mahdieh Soleymani Baghshah
- **Abstract**: This paper presents a comprehensive evaluation framework for aligning Persian Large Language Models (LLMs) with critical ethical dimensions, including safety, fairness, and social norms. It addresses the gaps in existing LLM evaluation frameworks by adapting them to Persian linguistic and cultural contexts. This benchmark creates three types of Persian-language benchmarks: (i) translated data, (ii) new data generated synthetically, and (iii) new naturally collected data. We translate Anthropic Red Teaming data, AdvBench, HarmBench, and DecodingTrust into Persian. Furthermore, we create ProhibiBench-fa, SafeBench-fa, FairBench-fa, and SocialBench-fa as new datasets to address harmful and prohibited content in indigenous culture. Moreover, we collect extensive dataset as GuardBench-fa to consider Persian cultural norms. By combining these datasets, our work establishes a unified framework for evaluating Persian LLMs, offering a new approach to culturally grounded alignment evaluation. A systematic evaluation of Persian LLMs is performed across the three alignment aspects: safety (avoiding harmful content), fairness (mitigating biases), and social norms (adhering to culturally accepted behaviors). We present a publicly available leaderboard that benchmarks Persian LLMs with respect to safety, fairness, and social norms at: https://huggingface.co/spaces/MCILAB/LLM_Alignment_Evaluation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "ELAB: Extensive LLM Alignment Benchmark in Persian Language" addresses the need for culturally relevant alignment benchmarks for Persian Large Language Models (LLMs).  It recognizes that existing alignment frameworks are primarily designed for English and do not adequately capture the nuances of Persian language and culture, which can lead to biased or harmful outputs.  The paper introduces a comprehensive evaluation framework comprising:

1.  **Translated Datasets:** Persian versions of established alignment datasets like Anthropic's red-teaming data, AdvBench, HarmBench, and DecodingTrust.
2.  **New Persian-Specific Datasets:**  ProhibiBench-fa, SafeBench-fa, FairBench-fa, SocialBench-fa, and GuardBench-fa, designed to address ethical issues and cultural norms unique to the Persian context (e.g., "taarof," "aberoo," dialect fairness).  These datasets cover prohibited content, safety, fairness, and social norms.
3.  **Unified Evaluation Framework:** A system for evaluating Persian LLMs across safety, fairness, and social norms, including a public leaderboard.
4. A scoring and evaluation process using LLM-as-a-judge.

The authors evaluate several LLMs using this framework.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The creation of Persian-specific alignment benchmarks is a valuable and novel contribution. Adapting existing English benchmarks through translation is helpful, but the truly significant novelty lies in the *design* of the new datasets (ProhibiBench-fa, SafeBench-fa, etc.) to capture Persian cultural norms and ethical considerations. This moves beyond simple translation and represents a genuine effort to address the unique challenges of aligning LLMs in a non-Western cultural context. The LLM-as-a-judge approach, while increasingly common, is applied in a novel context (Persian alignment) and combined with newly created benchmarks.

*   **Significance:** This work is significant for several reasons:

    *   **Addresses a Gap:**  It fills a critical gap in LLM alignment research by focusing on a non-English language and culture.  The paper convincingly argues that Western-centric frameworks are insufficient.
    *   **Promotes Responsible AI:**  It contributes to the responsible development of LLMs by ensuring they are aligned with the ethical and cultural values of Persian-speaking communities.
    *   **Provides a Framework:** It provides a structured and scalable evaluation framework that can be adapted to other under-represented languages.
    *   **Encourages Further Research:** By releasing the datasets and leaderboard, the paper encourages further research in Persian LLM alignment and multilingual AI ethics.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Approach:**  The framework covers multiple aspects of alignment (safety, fairness, social norms).
    *   **Culturally Grounded:** The new datasets are carefully designed to reflect Persian culture and values.
    *   **Practical Contribution:**  The leaderboard provides a tangible way to compare the alignment performance of different Persian LLMs.
    *   **Clear and Well-Written:** The paper is generally well-written and easy to understand.
    *   The use of the LLM-as-a-judge approach is well-justified, particularly with the inclusion of strong models such as GPT as evaluators.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Model Scope:** The evaluation focuses on relatively small LLMs (fewer than 10 billion parameters) due to computational constraints. Evaluating larger, more powerful models would provide a more comprehensive picture.
    *   **Subjectivity in Labeling:** While manual review and GPT-40-mini are used for labeling, there is still potential for subjectivity in classifying data as harmful, biased, or non-compliant with social norms.  A more rigorous inter-annotator agreement analysis could strengthen this aspect.
    *   **Depth of Cultural Nuance:** While the paper introduces datasets tailored to Persian cultural norms, capturing the full depth and complexity of these nuances is challenging. Further qualitative analysis of model outputs might reveal areas where alignment is still lacking. The 'cultural' dataset is the only one collected, which could be seen as a potential weakness, it seems more effort was placed on generated adversarial datasets instead of collecting more cultural data.

*   **Potential Influence:**  This paper is likely to have a significant influence on the field of multilingual AI ethics. It sets a precedent for developing culturally relevant alignment benchmarks for other under-represented languages and encourages researchers to move beyond simple translation approaches. The public leaderboard will also drive progress in Persian LLM alignment by fostering competition among model developers.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper is a solid contribution to the field of multilingual AI and responsible LLM development. The creation of the Persian-specific benchmarks is a valuable and novel effort. While the study has some limitations in terms of model scope and potential subjectivity in labeling, the strengths of the work far outweigh its weaknesses. The paper's potential influence on the field is substantial.

### Benchmarking LLM-based Relevance Judgment Methods
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12558v1)
- **Authors**: Negar Arabzadeh, Charles L. A. Clarke
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed in both academic and industry settings to automate the evaluation of information seeking systems, particularly by generating graded relevance judgments. Previous work on LLM-based relevance assessment has primarily focused on replicating graded human relevance judgments through various prompting strategies. However, there has been limited exploration of alternative assessment methods or comprehensive comparative studies. In this paper, we systematically compare multiple LLM-based relevance assessment methods, including binary relevance judgments, graded relevance assessments, pairwise preference-based methods, and two nugget-based evaluation methods~--~document-agnostic and document-dependent. In addition to a traditional comparison based on system rankings using Kendall correlations, we also examine how well LLM judgments align with human preferences, as inferred from relevance grades. We conduct extensive experiments on datasets from three TREC Deep Learning tracks 2019, 2020 and 2021 as well as the ANTIQUE dataset, which focuses on non-factoid open-domain question answering. As part of our data release, we include relevance judgments generated by both an open-source (Llama3.2b) and a commercial (gpt-4o) model. Our goal is to \textit{reproduce} various LLM-based relevance judgment methods to provide a comprehensive comparison. All code, data, and resources are publicly available in our GitHub Repository at https://github.com/Narabzad/llm-relevance-judgement-comparison.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Benchmarking LLM-based Relevance Judgment Methods" systematically compares different methods for using Large Language Models (LLMs) to automate relevance judgment in Information Retrieval (IR) evaluation. The authors compare binary relevance, graded relevance (using UMBRELLA), two nugget-based methods (Exam and AutoNuggetizer), and pairwise preference assessment. They use both a commercial (GPT-40) and an open-source LLM (Llama 3.2B) and benchmark on TREC Deep Learning datasets and the ANTIQUE dataset. The key focus is comparing the methods based on their alignment with human labels (document ordering) and agreement with system rankings (correlation with nDCG@10). The authors release all generated relevance judgments, code, and experimental results to facilitate future research. The paper finds that pairwise preferences offer high alignment with human judgments, while binary or graded relevance scores often correlate better with system rankings.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its **systematic and comparative** analysis of different LLM-based relevance judgment methods. Previous work has often focused on optimizing prompts for a single method (usually graded relevance). The paper fills a significant gap by providing a head-to-head comparison of these different methods. The introduction of an alignment measure with human document ordering, side-stepping absolute relevance judgements, is a reasonable contribution. The emphasis on making the work reproducible and reusable is also commendable.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial issue in modern IR evaluation: the increasing reliance on LLMs for relevance assessment and the need to understand the trade-offs between different methods. The findings are significant because they highlight that no single method is universally optimal. Pairwise preference judgements are shown to align well with human preferences, whereas binary judgements seem to have better alignment with existing test set relevance standards (in terms of system rankings), meaning that choosing an evaluation setup depends on the overall purpose. The findings provide practical guidance to researchers and practitioners on selecting the most appropriate LLM-based evaluation method for their specific needs.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Comparison:** The paper covers a range of diverse relevance assessment methods, providing a thorough evaluation landscape.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The release of all data, code, and resources is a significant strength, enabling future research and comparisons.
    *   **Two Evaluation Factors:** The use of both alignment with human labels and agreement with system rankings gives a holistic view of the strengths and weaknesses of each method.
    *   **Use of Established Tools**: Where possible the authors used existing tools and prompts, reducing the influence of prompt engineering.
    *   **Multiple Datasets:** Experiments across TREC DL and ANTIQUE increase generalizability.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Explanation of Discrepancies:** While the paper presents the results, it could delve deeper into explaining *why* certain methods perform better in certain situations. Is it inherent to the method itself or related to the nature of the dataset (factoid vs. non-factoid)?
    *   **LLM Specificity**: The experiments are done using GPT-4 and Llama, with Llama results relegated to the repository. Are the results general or specific to the LLMs used?
    *   **Limited Dataset Specificity:** All datasets used here are quite similar - spanning a range of information retrieval and question answering. While the authors do discuss differences in method success rates across datasets, the paper lacks a study of how retrieval/question types influence the performance of each method (a deeper study of dataset-specific behaviours).

*   **Justification of Score:** The paper makes a valuable contribution by providing a much-needed comparative analysis of different LLM-based relevance judgment methods. The emphasis on reproducibility is commendable, and the insights gained about the trade-offs between alignment and agreement are significant. While deeper analysis and extension to different LLMs would further strengthen the work, the paper clearly advances the field of automated IR evaluation and provides a strong foundation for future research.

### CDF-RAG: Causal Dynamic Feedback for Adaptive Retrieval-Augmented Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12560v1)
- **Authors**: Elahe Khatibi, Ziyu Wang, Amir M. Rahmani
- **Abstract**: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) has significantly enhanced large language models (LLMs) in knowledge-intensive tasks by incorporating external knowledge retrieval. However, existing RAG frameworks primarily rely on semantic similarity and correlation-driven retrieval, limiting their ability to distinguish true causal relationships from spurious associations. This results in responses that may be factually grounded but fail to establish cause-and-effect mechanisms, leading to incomplete or misleading insights. To address this issue, we introduce Causal Dynamic Feedback for Adaptive Retrieval-Augmented Generation (CDF-RAG), a framework designed to improve causal consistency, factual accuracy, and explainability in generative reasoning. CDF-RAG iteratively refines queries, retrieves structured causal graphs, and enables multi-hop causal reasoning across interconnected knowledge sources. Additionally, it validates responses against causal pathways, ensuring logically coherent and factually grounded outputs. We evaluate CDF-RAG on four diverse datasets, demonstrating its ability to improve response accuracy and causal correctness over existing RAG-based methods. Our code is publicly available at https://github.com/ elakhatibi/CDF-RAG.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary**

The paper introduces CDF-RAG (Causal Dynamic Feedback for Adaptive Retrieval-Augmented Generation), a new framework designed to improve causal consistency, factual accuracy, and explainability in generative reasoning for LLMs. The core idea is to enhance Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) by incorporating causal reasoning. CDF-RAG refines user queries using reinforcement learning (RL), retrieves knowledge through a dual-path mechanism (semantic vector search and causal graph traversal), and then validates generated responses against causal pathways to ensure logical coherence and factual grounding. The authors evaluate CDF-RAG on four QA datasets, demonstrating improvements in accuracy and causal correctness compared to existing RAG methods.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The key novelty lies in the integration of several components to address the specific problem of causal reasoning within RAG systems. While components such as query refinement, causal graph retrieval, and hallucination detection have been explored in isolation, the authors have combined these in a novel feedback loop.  The RL-based query refinement tailored for causal retrieval is another notable contribution. While none of these components are entirely groundbreaking on their own, the synergistic combination is innovative and valuable.
*   **Significance:** Existing RAG systems often struggle with tasks requiring deeper understanding and causal inference, leading to responses that may be factually correct but lack explanatory power. CDF-RAG tackles this limitation directly by incorporating causal reasoning into the retrieval and generation processes. This enhancement is particularly relevant for applications requiring a high degree of trustworthiness and interpretability, such as medical decision-making.  The paper also shows how to dynamically adapt retrieval strategies to enhance causal reasoning.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   The integrated framework addresses a clear and important limitation of standard RAG systems.
    *   The experimental results demonstrate consistent improvements across diverse datasets and LLM backbones. The inclusion of datasets CosmosQA, MedQA, MedMCQA, and AdversarialQA is broad enough to give confidence in the robustness of the method
    *   The ablation study provides insights into the contribution of each component in the framework, illustrating the value of their integration.
    *   The case study provides an illustrative overview of the operationalization of the CDF-RAG system

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The framework's reliance on a causal knowledge graph is a limitation. The construction of this graph from text data using UniCausal and subsequent validation with GPT-4 introduces potential for errors and biases. The approach to verifying extracted causal claims can also be computationally expensive. Also, the causal graph must be complete which is unlikely in the real world.
    *   The RL-based query refinement is potentially computationally intensive during training.
    *   While the experimental results are promising, further analysis of the failure cases of CDF-RAG would strengthen the paper. Understanding when and why CDF-RAG fails would further contribute to the knowledge of the limitations of the approach.
    *   The use of GPT-4 for several tasks, like prompt refinement and causal validity validation adds computational overhead, although it improves accuracy.
*   **Potential Influence:** CDF-RAG provides a strong foundation for future research in causality-aware RAG systems. The framework and its components can be further developed and adapted for various applications that require high trustworthiness and interpretability. The integration of RL for query refinement in RAG systems has a high potential of influencing the development of agentic and dynamic RAG architectures.

**Justification of Score:**

Given the novel integration of various components, the robust experimental evaluation, and the identified limitations, a score of 7 is justified. The paper presents a significant contribution to the field of RAG systems by directly addressing the critical problem of causal reasoning. The integrated framework and the RL-based query refinement are valuable additions to the toolset for enhanced RAG systems. While the dependency on a causal knowledge graph and the computational cost associated with RL training present limitations, the potential impact of CDF-RAG on enhancing trustworthiness and explainability in generative reasoning makes it a substantial contribution.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### ZeroSumEval: Scaling LLM Evaluation with Inter-Model Competition
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12562v1)
- **Authors**: Haidar Khan, Hisham A. Alyahya, Yazeed Alnumay, M Saiful Bari, Bülent Yener
- **Abstract**: Evaluating the capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) has traditionally relied on static benchmark datasets, human assessments, or model-based evaluations - methods that often suffer from overfitting, high costs, and biases. ZeroSumEval is a novel competition-based evaluation protocol that leverages zero-sum games to assess LLMs with dynamic benchmarks that resist saturation. ZeroSumEval encompasses a diverse suite of games, including security challenges (PyJail), classic games (Chess, Liar's Dice, Poker), knowledge tests (MathQuiz), and persuasion challenges (Gandalf, Debate). These games are designed to evaluate a range of AI capabilities such as strategic reasoning, planning, knowledge application, and creativity. Building upon recent studies that highlight the effectiveness of game-based evaluations for LLMs, ZeroSumEval enhances these approaches by providing a standardized and extensible framework. To demonstrate this, we conduct extensive experiments with >7000 simulations across 7 games and 13 models. Our results show that while frontier models from the GPT and Claude families can play common games and answer questions, they struggle to play games that require creating novel and challenging questions. We also observe that models cannot reliably jailbreak each other and fail generally at tasks requiring creativity. We release our code at https://github.com/facebookresearch/ZeroSumEval.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the "ZeroSumEval: Scaling LLM Evaluation with Inter-Model Competition" paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ZeroSumEval, a novel evaluation framework for Large Language Models (LLMs) that uses competition-based, zero-sum games. It aims to address the limitations of traditional LLM evaluation methods, like overfitting, high costs, and biases, by creating dynamic benchmarks that resist saturation. ZeroSumEval encompasses a diverse suite of games including security challenges (PyJail), classic games (Chess, Liar's Dice, Poker), knowledge tests (MathQuiz), and persuasion challenges (Gandalf, Debate). The paper presents experimental results from over 7000 simulations across 7 games and 13 models demonstrating that while some frontier models perform well on common games and answering questions, they struggle with tasks that require creating novel content, reliably jailbreaking other models, and general creativity. The framework leverages DSPy for prompt abstraction and feedback loops, and the Bradley-Terry rating system for model ranking.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using zero-sum games for LLM evaluation has some precedent in related literature but ZeroSumEval makes a significant contribution by packaging this concept into a standardized, extensible, and automated framework. The specific games selected, particularly the generation-based ones (MathQuiz, PyJail), offer a fresh approach to evaluating LLM capabilities beyond simple question answering or text generation. However, the use of games is not entirely new, existing frameworks tackle similar concepts such as chat arena.

*   **Significance:** Addressing the limitations of static benchmarks is crucial for the continued advancement of LLMs. ZeroSumEval's dynamic, competition-based approach offers a promising alternative that mitigates issues like data contamination and saturation. The paper attempts to provide a method that scales with LLM capabilities as better models will presumably be able to both defend and attack better. By evaluating capabilities such as strategic reasoning, knowledge application, and creativity through game play, the framework goes beyond assessing simple factual recall and helps uncover more complex, human-like traits of LLMs.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Extensibility and Standardization:** The framework is designed to be easily extensible with new games and models, allowing for continuous evaluation and adaptation to evolving LLM capabilities.
    *   **Automated Evaluation:** The automated manager, particularly for generation-based games, provides a scalable alternative to human evaluation.
    *   **Robustness to Prompting:**  The prompt abstraction through DSPy reduces reliance on manual prompt engineering and improves consistency across models.
    *   **Informative Results:** The experimental results provide valuable insights into the strengths and weaknesses of various LLM architectures, particularly in generation/security related tasks.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Computational Cost:** Running extensive simulations across multiple models and games can be computationally expensive, potentially limiting accessibility for smaller research groups.
    *   **Reliance on Game Rules:** The framework's effectiveness depends on the design and balance of the games themselves. A poorly designed game may not accurately reflect the desired LLM capabilities or may introduce unintended biases.
    *   **Limited "Real-World" Applicability:** While ZeroSumEval helps evaluate LLMs through game play, the link to practical, real-world applications of the skills being tested may not always be direct.
    *   **Limited Insight:** Evaluation of general LLM capabilities are based on games, lacking insight on other important capabilities such as summarizing and translation.
    *   **Game Specific Metrics:** The rating system and interpretation might be highly game dependent. ZeroSumEval does not provide insight on how to weigh the different games for the models' final ranking.
    *   **Potential Bias:** Despite abstracting the prompt, this does not completely remove prompting sensitivities to different models.

*   **Impact:** The release of ZeroSumEval could encourage further research into competition-based evaluation methods and the development of more challenging benchmarks. The insights gained from the framework can inform the design and training of future LLMs with improved strategic reasoning, creativity, and security. However, the complexity of setup and the computational cost may limit its widespread adoption.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:**

ZeroSumEval presents a solid, well-executed contribution with meaningful novelty. The system aims to solve an important problem and takes a compelling approach.
The framework tackles critical issues in LLM evaluation and offers a standardized, scalable alternative to static benchmarks. The approach tackles known shortcomings of LLMs that are difficult to assess by static benchmarks such as creativity, problem solving and strategic reasoning. The results presented provide valuable insights into model behavior and the code release makes it a valuable research tool. However, there are still questions regarding the reliance on game design, computational cost, and the direct link to real-world applications.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Prompt-Driven and Training-Free Forgetting Approach and Dataset for Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12574v1)
- **Authors**: Zhenyu Yu, Mohd Yamani Inda Idris, Pei Wang
- **Abstract**: The widespread adoption of diffusion models in image generation has increased the demand for privacy-compliant unlearning. However, due to the high-dimensional nature and complex feature representations of diffusion models, achieving selective unlearning remains challenging, as existing methods struggle to remove sensitive information while preserving the consistency of non-sensitive regions. To address this, we propose an Automatic Dataset Creation Framework based on prompt-based layered editing and training-free local feature removal, constructing the ForgetMe dataset and introducing the Entangled evaluation metric. The Entangled metric quantifies unlearning effectiveness by assessing the similarity and consistency between the target and background regions and supports both paired (Entangled-D) and unpaired (Entangled-S) image data, enabling unsupervised evaluation. The ForgetMe dataset encompasses a diverse set of real and synthetic scenarios, including CUB-200-2011 (Birds), Stanford-Dogs, ImageNet, and a synthetic cat dataset. We apply LoRA fine-tuning on Stable Diffusion to achieve selective unlearning on this dataset and validate the effectiveness of both the ForgetMe dataset and the Entangled metric, establishing them as benchmarks for selective unlearning. Our work provides a scalable and adaptable solution for advancing privacy-preserving generative AI.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a prompt-driven, training-free approach for selective forgetting in large generative models, specifically diffusion models.  It addresses the challenge of removing sensitive information while preserving the consistency of non-sensitive regions. The core contributions are: 1) an automatic dataset creation framework that utilizes prompt-based layered editing and training-free local feature removal; 2) the *ForgetMe* dataset, comprising diverse real and synthetic images for evaluating selective unlearning; and 3) the *Entangled* metric, which quantifies unlearning effectiveness by assessing the similarity and consistency between target and background regions, supporting both paired and unpaired image data. The paper demonstrates the effectiveness of the *ForgetMe* dataset and *Entangled* metric through LoRA fine-tuning on Stable Diffusion and extensive experiments.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the comprehensive approach to selective unlearning, encompassing dataset creation, a novel evaluation metric, and a training-free removal framework tailored for diffusion models. The *Entangled* metric, which combines similarity and consistency, is a significant improvement over existing metrics that primarily focus on data removal without considering background preservation. The automatic dataset creation process allows for scalable generation of appropriate data for unlearning research. The concept of layer-based editing is not entirely novel, but its application in a training-free removal scheme to prepare unlearning data adds value.

*   **Significance:** The work is significant as it directly addresses a crucial issue in the generative AI field: privacy compliance and ethical considerations regarding sensitive data retention.  The creation of a standardized dataset and a well-defined metric fills a gap in the current research landscape, enabling more robust and reliable evaluation of unlearning methods. The training-free aspect of the dataset creation framework reduces computational burden, making the approach practical. The paper’s results demonstrate tangible improvements over existing object removal methods by maintaining generation quality while effectively removing target concepts. The work has a clear focus with a well-defined problem, solid contributions and experimental validation.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Approach:** The combination of dataset creation, a novel metric, and a removal framework provides a holistic solution.
    *   **Practicality:** The training-free nature of the dataset creation is a significant advantage.
    *   **Quantitative Evaluation:** Extensive experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed method and metric.
    *   **Focus and Clarity:** The paper is well-structured and clearly articulates its goals and contributions.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limitations of Object Removal:** The paper acknowledges limitations in handling transparent objects and potential visual discontinuities when merging layers from different sources.
    *   **Prompt Dependence:** The reliance on prompt engineering for object removal can be a weakness, as subtle changes in prompts can affect results. The object removal and dataset creation framework is prompt dependent, and results may vary for different prompt configurations. The quality of the created unlearning dataset also relies on the capability of the underlying large models (such as Stable Diffusion) being used.
    *   **Limited Baseline Comparison:** The study mainly compares against one major baseline, which might leave the space for additional comparison with more unlearning models for the generative space.

*   **Potential Influence:** The *ForgetMe* dataset and *Entangled* metric are likely to become valuable benchmarks for future research in selective unlearning for generative models.  The training-free removal framework offers a practical solution for generating unlearning datasets, facilitating further development and comparison of unlearning algorithms.

**Overall, the paper makes a significant contribution to the field by addressing a practical and pressing issue in generative AI and providing a well-defined dataset and evaluation metric to facilitate future research. The limitations are clearly acknowledged, and the strengths significantly outweigh the weaknesses.**

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:** The paper is a solid piece of research with valuable practical contributions. It introduces a comprehensive approach to a significant problem and provides a readily usable dataset and metric. The score is not higher because of the known limitations (especially the prompt dependence and the lack of exhaustive comparisons with more generative-model specific unlearning strategies).

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Identifying and Mitigating the Influence of the Prior Distribution in Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12585v1)
- **Authors**: Liyi Zhang, Veniamin Veselovsky, R. Thomas McCoy, Thomas L. Griffiths
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) sometimes fail to respond appropriately to deterministic tasks -- such as counting or forming acronyms -- because the implicit prior distribution they have learned over sequences of tokens influences their responses. In this work, we show that, in at least some cases, LLMs actually compute the information needed to perform these tasks correctly, and we identify some interventions that can allow them to access this information to improve their performance. First, we show that simply prompting the language model to not rely on its prior knowledge leads to dramatic improvements in prior-dominated tasks. We then use mechanistic interpretability techniques to localize the prior within the LLM and manipulate the extent to which that prior influences its responses. Specifically, we show that it is possible to identify layers of the underlying neural network that correlate with the prior probability of a response and that lightweight finetuning of these layers with basic prompts on prior-dominated tasks achieves high performance on held-out answers. These results suggest that the information required to produce a correct response is contained within the representations of the problems formed by the models. Furthermore, we show that this finetuning is significantly more effective for prior-dominated tasks, and that the error after finetuning is no longer correlated with the prior. Our results suggest that it may be possible to define effective methods for manipulating the extent to which LLMs rely upon their priors in solving problems, potentially increasing their performance in settings where LLMs hallucinate for reasons related to the prior probability of token sequences.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates how the implicit prior distribution learned by large language models (LLMs) influences their performance on deterministic tasks (e.g., counting, creating acronyms). It demonstrates that LLMs sometimes fail to produce correct responses because they are biased towards high-probability token sequences, even when they have the necessary information to solve the task correctly.  The authors identify layers in LLMs correlating with prior probability and demonstrate interventions, including simple prompt engineering (asking the model not to rely on its prior) and stratified finetuning, that can significantly improve performance by reducing the influence of the prior. The stratified finetuning is significantly more effective for prior-dominated tasks, suggesting a possible method for manipulating LLMs' reliance on their priors.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper builds upon existing work identifying the influence of priors on LLM performance (McCoy et al., 2023) but goes further in several significant ways. Most importantly, it identifies mechanisms within the model and provides interventions to mitigate the negative effects of the prior. Identifying specific layers correlated with the prior and showing that finetuning those layers improves performance is a substantial contribution. This mechanistic interpretability approach, combined with the demonstrated interventions, marks a clear step forward.  The stratified finetuning approach is a clever way to avoid overfitting and ensure that the model is actually learning to solve the task rather than simply memorizing answers. The 'do not rely on prior knowledge' prompt engineering is surprisingly effective and offers a practical, low-cost method for improving performance in some situations.
*   **Significance:** The paper's findings are significant for several reasons. First, it provides a deeper understanding of why LLMs fail in certain deterministic tasks, moving beyond simply observing the failure to understanding the underlying mechanisms. Second, it offers concrete strategies for mitigating these failures, potentially improving the reliability and accuracy of LLMs in various applications.  The ability to manipulate the extent to which LLMs rely on their priors could be particularly valuable in settings where LLMs are prone to hallucination or bias. The work is important for researchers working to understand and improve the reliability of LLMs, with applications in question answering, code generation, and other areas where deterministic behavior is desired.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of prior-dominated errors in LLMs.
    *   **Mechanistic Insights:** The identification of specific layers encoding the prior is a valuable contribution.
    *   **Effective Interventions:** The demonstrated interventions (prompt engineering and stratified finetuning) are practical and effective.
    *   **Rigorous Evaluation:** The stratified train/validation split is a robust method for evaluating the effectiveness of the interventions.
    *   **Well-written and Clear:** The paper is well-written and easy to understand.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Task Scope:** The study focuses on a relatively small set of deterministic tasks. It would be valuable to explore whether the findings generalize to other types of tasks where priors may be influential.
    *   **Model Specificity:** While the paper uses LLAMA 3, the transferability of the identified layers and optimal finetuning strategies to other LLM architectures needs to be investigated.
    *   **Limited Exploration of Linear Intervention Methods**: These are dismissed quickly in the appendix because they did not lead to significant improvements.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in several areas:
    *   Mechanistic interpretability of LLMs
    *   Development of methods for mitigating prior-related biases and hallucinations
    *   Design of LLM architectures that are less susceptible to prior influence

**Justification for Score:**

This paper makes a notable contribution to understanding and mitigating the influence of priors in LLMs. While the scope is somewhat limited to specific tasks and a single model architecture, the mechanistic insights and effective interventions are significant. The stratified finetuning approach and the surprisingly effective prompt engineering offer practical solutions.  Therefore, it has high potential for future development.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Simplifying Graph Transformers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12588v1)
- **Authors**: Liheng Ma, Soumyasundar Pal, Yingxue Zhang, Philip H. S. Torr, Mark Coates
- **Abstract**: Transformers have attained outstanding performance across various modalities, employing scaled-dot-product (SDP) attention mechanisms. Researchers have attempted to migrate Transformers to graph learning, but most advanced Graph Transformers are designed with major architectural differences, either integrating message-passing or incorporating sophisticated attention mechanisms. These complexities prevent the easy adoption of Transformer training advances. We propose three simple modifications to the plain Transformer to render it applicable to graphs without introducing major architectural distortions. Specifically, we advocate for the use of (1) simplified $L_2$ attention to measure the magnitude closeness of tokens; (2) adaptive root-mean-square normalization to preserve token magnitude information; and (3) a relative positional encoding bias with a shared encoder. Significant performance gains across a variety of graph datasets justify the effectiveness of our proposed modifications. Furthermore, empirical evaluation on the expressiveness benchmark reveals noteworthy realized expressiveness in the graph isomorphism.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of adapting Transformer architectures for graph learning.  Existing Graph Transformers often deviate significantly from the original Transformer design, incorporating complex components like message-passing networks (MPNNs) or sophisticated attention mechanisms. This hinders the transfer of advancements from standard Transformers and obscures the potential for multi-modal unification. The authors propose three simple modifications to a plain Transformer to make it suitable for graph data: (1) simplified L2 attention to measure token magnitude closeness, (2) adaptive root-mean-square normalization (AdaRMSN) to preserve token magnitude information, and (3) relative positional encoding with a shared encoder.  They demonstrate empirically that these modifications lead to performance gains on several graph datasets and improve expressiveness in graph isomorphism testing.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper presents a moderate level of novelty. While applying Transformers to graphs isn't new, the focus on *simplifying* and *adapting* the core Transformer architecture, rather than fundamentally redesigning it, is a valuable contribution. The specific modifications – L2 attention, AdaRMSN, and the shared encoder in positional encoding – are individually incremental, but their combination and the justification for their use within the context of graph learning represent a coherent and potentially impactful approach. The AdaRMSN is probably the most novel single component. The shared encoder in positional encoding isn't revolutionary but aligns with the goal of simplifying and leveraging existing Transformer components.

* **Significance:** The significance of the work lies in its potential to bridge the gap between advancements in standard Transformers and graph learning.  By retaining the plain Transformer architecture, the proposed modifications make it easier to incorporate future improvements from language or vision domains into graph learning models.  The demonstrated empirical gains on benchmark datasets further support the practical value of the approach. However, the extent to which these modifications truly "unlock" the full potential of standard Transformers for graphs remains to be seen and requires further research and more comprehensive benchmarks. A limitation in demonstrating significance would be the reliance on relatively simple or smaller datasets compared to the massive datasets used for pretraining in NLP or vision.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the drawbacks of complex Graph Transformer architectures.
    * **Simple and Effective Modifications:** The proposed modifications are conceptually simple and easy to implement.
    * **Empirical Validation:** The paper provides empirical results on multiple datasets, demonstrating the effectiveness of the approach.
    * **Expressiveness Analysis:**  The inclusion of expressiveness analysis on the BREC benchmark is a valuable addition, providing insights beyond standard dataset performance.
    * **Focus on Bridging the Gap:** The paper's focus on bridging the gap between standard Transformers and Graph Transformers is a valuable goal for the field.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Incremental Novelty of Individual Components:** The individual components (L2 Attention, AdaRMSN) are somewhat incremental on existing techniques.
    * **Limited Dataset Scale:** The empirical evaluations are performed on datasets smaller in scale than those used in large-scale Transformer pretraining in other domains. This limits the assessment of how well the proposed method scales to very large graphs.
    * **Depth of Theoretical Analysis:** The theoretical analysis of the proposed modifications could be strengthened.
    * **The reliance on RRWP:** This can be seen as limitation (although the authors state that the SGT approach can be generalized to other forms of PE).
    * **Dependence on "tricks":** For example, the authors include "a BN in the prediction head post-graph-pooling effectively mitigates the problem."
    * **Limited exploration of other recent Transformer architectures:** While the authors focus on plain transformers, an interesting direction would be to consider whether SGT architectures could work alongside architectural tricks from other Transformers.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by encouraging researchers to explore simpler and more adaptable Graph Transformer architectures. It could lead to a more unified approach to Transformer design across different data modalities. However, its impact will depend on future work building upon these modifications and demonstrating their effectiveness on larger and more complex graph datasets.

**Score: 6.5**

**Justification:**

The paper offers a valuable and well-motivated simplification to Graph Transformers.  While the individual modifications are incremental, their combination addresses a clear problem within the field, and the empirical results are promising.  The paper's significance is primarily in its potential to facilitate the transfer of advancements from standard Transformers to graph learning and foster a more unified approach to Transformer design. The limitations in dataset scale and the depth of theoretical analysis prevent it from achieving a higher score. It is above average and worthwhile but not fundamentally revolutionary and could benefit from further validation and more in-depth analysis in future work.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### GeoSense: Evaluating Identification and Application of Geometric Principles in Multimodal Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12597v1)
- **Authors**: Liangyu Xu, Yingxiu Zhao, Jingyun Wang, Yingyao Wang, Bu Pi, Chen Wang, Mingliang Zhang, Jihao Gu, Xiang Li, Xiaoyong Zhu, Jun Song, Bo Zheng
- **Abstract**: Geometry problem-solving (GPS), a challenging task requiring both visual comprehension and symbolic reasoning, effectively measures the reasoning capabilities of multimodal large language models (MLLMs). Humans exhibit strong reasoning ability in this task through accurate identification and adaptive application of geometric principles within visual contexts. However, existing benchmarks fail to jointly assess both dimensions of the human-like geometric reasoning mechanism in MLLMs, remaining a critical gap in assessing their ability to tackle GPS. To this end, we introduce GeoSense, the first comprehensive bilingual benchmark designed to systematically evaluate the geometric reasoning abilities of MLLMs through the lens of geometric principles. GeoSense features a five-level hierarchical framework of geometric principles spanning plane and solid geometry, an intricately annotated dataset of 1,789 problems, and an innovative evaluation strategy. Through extensive experiments on GeoSense with various open-source and closed-source MLLMs, we observe that Gemini-2.0-pro-flash performs best, achieving an overall score of $65.3$. Our in-depth analysis reveals that the identification and application of geometric principles remain a bottleneck for leading MLLMs, jointly hindering their reasoning abilities. These findings underscore GeoSense's potential to guide future advancements in MLLMs' geometric reasoning capabilities, paving the way for more robust and human-like reasoning in artificial intelligence.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "GeoSense: Evaluating Identification and Application of Geometric Principles in Multimodal Reasoning":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces GeoSense, a new bilingual benchmark designed to evaluate the geometric reasoning abilities of multimodal large language models (MLLMs). It addresses a gap in existing benchmarks by focusing on two key aspects of human-like geometric reasoning: the accurate identification of relevant geometric principles and their correct application within visual contexts. GeoSense features a hierarchical framework of 148 geometric principles, a dataset of 1,789 problems with detailed annotations, and novel evaluation metrics (GPI and GPA) for assessing principle identification and application, along with overall answer accuracy. Experiments conducted with various MLLMs reveal that while some models excel in computation, identifying and applying geometric principles, particularly in plane geometry, remains a challenge. The paper highlights Gemini-2.0-Pro-Flash as the best-performing model, but notes that even it has limitations in adaptively applying principles.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its integrated evaluation approach. While existing benchmarks address geometric problem-solving, they often focus solely on final answer accuracy or partial reasoning steps. GeoSense uniquely combines the assessment of geometric principle identification and application, offering a more holistic view of MLLM capabilities. The creation of a hierarchical framework of geometric principles and the detailed annotation of the dataset, including linking principles to visual elements, are also valuable contributions.

**Significance:** GeoSense fills a crucial gap in the evaluation of MLLMs for geometric reasoning. By explicitly measuring the identification and application of geometric principles, the benchmark provides targeted insights into the areas where MLLMs struggle. This information is highly valuable for guiding future research aimed at improving MLLMs' geometric reasoning skills. The benchmark's bilingual nature and detailed annotations also enhance its accessibility and utility.  The finding that even advanced MLLMs struggle with correctly applying geometric principles despite demonstrating some proficiency in identifying them highlights the importance of the proposed GeoSense approach. The paper's analysis of error types also offers actionable insights for improving MLLM architectures and training strategies.

**Strengths:**

*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper introduces a comprehensive and novel framework for evaluating geometric reasoning.
*   **Detailed Annotation:** The dataset is meticulously annotated, linking geometric principles to visual elements.
*   **Actionable Insights:** The experimental results and error analysis provide valuable insights for future research.
*   **Bilingual Support:** The benchmark supports both English and Chinese, enhancing its accessibility.
* The experimental design is comprehensive covering a range of open and closed source MLLMs.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Reliance on GPT-4 for Annotation:** The paper relies on GPT-4 for generating initial annotations and extracting geometric principles. While human experts review and correct the output, the potential for bias or inaccuracies introduced by GPT-4 remains a concern. Further work could involve comparisons with human only based annotations.
*   **Complexity of Evaluation Metrics:** The GPA score, while innovative, could be considered complex. A more intuitive or simpler metric might improve the usability of the benchmark.
*   The paper could benefit from a more extensive discussion of the limitations of the benchmark itself. This would involve outlining assumptions made during construction, and highlighting potential areas where GeoSense might not accurately assess reasoning capabilities.

**Potential Impact:** GeoSense has the potential to become a standard benchmark for evaluating geometric reasoning in MLLMs. Its detailed evaluation framework and actionable insights can significantly influence the development of more robust and human-like AI systems capable of tackling complex visual reasoning tasks.

**Rigorous Rationale:**

The paper presents a significant contribution to the field by providing a novel and comprehensive framework for evaluating geometric reasoning in MLLMs. The detailed annotations, hierarchical framework of geometric principles, and the focus on principle identification and application distinguish it from existing benchmarks. While the reliance on GPT-4 for annotation and the complexity of evaluation metrics represent minor weaknesses, the paper's strengths significantly outweigh its limitations. The insights gleaned from the experiments and error analysis are particularly valuable for guiding future research in this area. The potential influence of GeoSense on the development of more capable and human-like AI systems justifies a high score.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Code Copycat Conundrum: Demystifying Repetition in LLM-based Code Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12608v1)
- **Authors**: Mingwei Liu, Juntao Li, Ying Wang, Xueying Du, Zuoyu Ou, Qiuyuan Chen, Bingxu An, Zhao Wei, Yong Xu, Fangming Zou, Xin Peng, Yiling Lou
- **Abstract**: Despite recent advances in Large Language Models (LLMs) for code generation, the quality of LLM-generated code still faces significant challenges. One significant issue is code repetition, which refers to the model's tendency to generate structurally redundant code, resulting in inefficiencies and reduced readability. To address this, we conduct the first empirical study to investigate the prevalence and nature of repetition across 19 state-of-the-art code LLMs using three widely-used benchmarks. Our study includes both quantitative and qualitative analyses, revealing that repetition is pervasive and manifests at various granularities and extents, including character, statement, and block levels. We further summarize a taxonomy of 20 repetition patterns. Building on our findings, we propose DeRep, a rule-based technique designed to detect and mitigate repetition in generated code. We evaluate DeRep using both open-source benchmarks and in an industrial setting. Our results demonstrate that DeRep significantly outperforms baselines in reducing repetition (with an average improvements of 91.3%, 93.5%, and 79.9% in rep-3, rep-line, and sim-line metrics) and enhancing code quality (with a Pass@1 increase of 208.3% over greedy search). Furthermore, integrating DeRep improves the performance of existing repetition mitigation methods, with Pass@1 improvements ranging from 53.7% to 215.7%.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Code Copycat Conundrum: Demystifying Repetition in LLM-based Code Generation" investigates the problem of code repetition in code generated by Large Language Models (LLMs). It presents an empirical study analyzing the prevalence and nature of repetition across various code LLMs using common benchmarks. The authors identify and categorize 20 distinct repetition patterns, encompassing different levels of granularity (character, statement, block) and extent. Based on these findings, they propose DeRep, a rule-based technique for detecting and mitigating repetition in LLM-generated code. They evaluate DeRep in both open-source benchmarks and an industrial setting, demonstrating its effectiveness in reducing repetition and improving code quality compared to baseline methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper addresses a relevant and practical problem in LLM-based code generation: code repetition. While repetition is a known issue in text generation, the paper makes a specific contribution by focusing on the unique challenges presented in *code* generation, considering the structured and often repetitive nature of programming. The identification and categorization of 20 distinct repetition patterns tailored for code is a valuable contribution. The proposal and evaluation of DeRep, specifically designed to address these patterns, adds to the novelty. The experimental setup, including both public benchmarks and industrial data, strengthens the study.

*   **Significance:** Code repetition negatively impacts code quality, readability, and efficiency. Addressing this problem is important for the widespread adoption and practical usability of LLM-generated code. The paper provides a systematic analysis of the problem, offering insights into its causes and characteristics. The proposed DeRep technique offers a practical solution that improves code quality and efficiency. The substantial performance improvements demonstrated by DeRep, particularly in terms of Pass@1 increase, are significant. The study is significant because it offers both a comprehensive analysis of a critical problem and a practical solution that can be readily integrated into existing code generation workflows.  The fact that it's tested in an industrial setting adds further weight to its practical importance.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Comprehensive Empirical Study: The paper performs a thorough empirical study, using a wide range of LLMs and benchmarks.
    *   Detailed Taxonomy: The taxonomy of repetition patterns is well-defined and provides a valuable framework for understanding the problem.
    *   Practical Solution: DeRep offers a practical, rule-based solution that is easy to implement and integrate with existing systems.
    *   Robust Evaluation: The evaluation includes both open-source benchmarks and an industrial setting, providing strong evidence of DeRep's effectiveness.
    *   Clear Writing: The paper is well-written and easy to follow.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Rule-Based Approach: DeRep relies on a rule-based approach, which may not be as adaptable to future LLMs or coding styles compared to a learned approach. While this provides simplicity, it raises questions about long-term maintainability and robustness.
    *   Limited Scope of Datasets: While the paper uses standard datasets, extending the analysis to cover more diverse and complex coding tasks would further strengthen its findings.
    *   Potential for Overfitting: The DeRep method, being rule-based, could be somewhat overfitted to the specific repetition patterns observed in the evaluated LLMs and datasets. A broader assessment with diverse LLMs and datasets could help to clarify this further.

*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to significantly impact the field of LLM-based code generation. The insights gained from the empirical study can inform the design of future code LLMs, and the DeRep technique can be used to improve the quality of code generated by existing LLMs.  The results could also motivate the development of more advanced, learning-based repetition mitigation techniques.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the above analysis, I assign a score of 7 to this paper. While the rule-based approach and the potential limitations related to overfitting might be considered drawbacks, the paper's core contributions outweigh these.
